However, even when considering a rebuttal of the presumption for a fair value hedge there are not many currencies with a liquid market for inflation-linked debt instruments, therefore further limiting the availability of designating non-contractually specified inflation risk components of financial instruments. In addition, even where the presumption is successfully rebutted, the other qualifying criteria must be met in order to apply hedge accounting (see section 6 below). In particular, demonstrating the existence of an economic relationship such that the hedged item and the hedging instrument have values that are generally expected to move in opposite directions could prove challenging, given all the fair value drivers for an inflation derivative. [IFRS 9.B6.4.4]. How we see it Following the April 2021 final agenda decision of the IFRS IC (see above), and in consideration of the existing rebuttable presumption that inflation is not separately identifiable in a financial instrument unless contractual, coupled with the difficulties of meeting the hedge effectiveness requirements in a fair value hedge of inflation risk (in particular the existence of an economic relationship), it is difficult to identify a fact pattern for which hedge accounting for non-contractual inflation risk in a financial instrument might be achieved. While IFRS 9 defines in what circumstances inflation can be a risk component for a financial instrument, inflation can be treated as a risk component for non-financial items under IFRS 9 in the same manner as any other risk component (as described in sections 2.2.2 and 2.2.3 above), i.e., the rebuttable presumption described in this section applies only to financial instruments. 2.2.7 Interest rate risk component in an insurance contract Entities that write longer term insurance contracts (typically life products paying annuities) often use derivatives to manage the interest rate risk they associate with those insurance liabilities. On application of IFRS 17 Insurance Contracts (see Chapter 51) insurance entities may be inclined to apply hedge accounting to their interest rate risk management derivatives in order to avoid an accounting mismatch and better represent risk management in their financial statements. IFRS 9 is silent on the application of hedge accounting to insurance contracts, hence the standard IFRS 9 hedge accounting criteria must be applied, in particular, the guidance on eligible hedged components. In order for a risk component to be eligible, the risk component must be separately identifiable and reliably measurable. This assessment must be made within the context of a particular market structure (see section 2.2.3.A above).
**[IFRS 9.B6.3.8, IFRS 9.B6.3.9].**
IFRS 9 provides guidance on how to determine whether a benchmark interest rate is separately identifiable and reliably measurable component of fixed rate debt within the market structure (see section 2.2.3.A above). [IFRS 9.B6.3.10(d)]. This guidance is also relevant in consideration of insurance contracts, however the bespoke and complex features often seen in insurance contracts make the guidance more difficult to interpret in the context of insurance contracts. Given the wide variety of insurance contracts, and their complex nature arising from various interrelated risk drivers, the assessment of whether a benchmark interest rate risk is eligible as a hedged component of an insurance contract in a fair value hedge will need to be undertaken for the particular facts and circumstances. Consideration as to what is the ‘particular market structure’ will also be important. Often there is a lack of a clear primary and, possibly, even a secondary market for insurance contracts (i.e., evidence suggests that in many markets, very few sales of insurance contracts occur post origination). Therefore, in some circumstances, it has been suggested that it could be appropriate to interpret ‘market structure’ as meaning the basis upon which market origination prices are established or the market conventions for fair value determination if such common market practices exist, perhaps driven by regulation. If an eligible benchmark interest rate component exists, there is an expectation it can be demonstrated, within the market structure, that changes in benchmark interest rates do have a determinable impact on the pricing and/or fair values of the particular insurance contracts. This is an area where practice could evolve in the future.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting When it has been determined that a benchmark interest rate component does exist within a group of insurance contracts, consideration must be given as to how lapse risk is incorporated into a hedge relationship (lapse risk is the risk that the actual behaviour of policy holders with respect to termination or breaking of insurance contracts is different to the assumptions used in pricing and managing those contracts). Like the identification of an eligible risk component, there will need to be an assessment of the relationship between lapse risk and benchmark interest rate risk within insurance contracts, taking into account the particular facts and circumstances to determine whether lapse risk is driven by changes in benchmark interest rate risk or not. If it is determined that lapse risk is driven by changes in benchmark interest rate risk, it must form part of the fair value adjustment. All the features of the hedged item whose fair value is affected by changes in the hedged risk must be included in the hedge relationship, otherwise the measurement of hedge effectiveness would not include the full fair value change with respect to the hedged risk. [IFRS 9.BC6.204]. Whether or not changes in lapse risk must be incorporated in the remeasurement of the hedged component within a fair value hedge relationship could have a significant impact on the levels of ineffectiveness (see section 7.1.1 below). Lapse risk within a portfolio of insurance contracts is comparable to pre-payment risk in a portfolio of fixed rate prepayable mortgages for interest rate risk management purposes. Despite the fact insurance contracts are outside the scope of IFRS 9, they are typically financial instruments so it makes some sense to apply a parallel treatment to lapse risk in a portfolio of insurance contracts to the treatment of prepayment risk in prepayable fixed rate mortgages within the portfolio fair value hedging guidance in IAS 39 (see section 10 below). In that case if it can be demonstrated that lapse risk is not driven by changes in benchmark interest rate, then changes in lapse risk would not form part of the gain or loss on the hedged risk.
**[IAS 39.AG121].**
If it is determined that lapse risk is not driven by changes in benchmark interest rate risk and so will not form part of the fair value hedge adjustment, changes in lapse risk could still result in a designated hedged item no longer existing. Therefore, there will be a need to track any hedge designations to ensure fair value hedge adjustments are accounted for appropriately, and in particular are released to profit or loss if the hedged item no longer exists (see sections 7.1.2 and 8.3.1 below). 2.3 Components of a nominal amount 2.3.1 General requirement A component of a nominal amount is a specified part of the amount of an item. [IFRS 9.6.3.7(c)]. This could be a proportion of an entire item (such as, 60% of a fixed rate loan of EUR100 million) or a layer component (for example, the ‘bottom layer’ of EUR60 million of a EUR100 million fixed rate loan that can be prepaid at fair value. ‘Bottom layer’ here refers to the portion of the loan that will be prepaid last). The type of component changes the accounting outcome. An entity must designate the component for accounting purposes consistently with its risk management objective (see section 6.2 below). [IFRS 9.B6.3.16, IFRS 9.B6.3.17]. A component must be less than the entire item, [IFRS 9.B6.3.7], and must be defined in such a way that it is possible to determine whether the usual effectiveness criteria are met and ongoing ineffectiveness can be measured. (See sections 6.4 and 7.4 respectively below). In particular the need for documented specificity of the hedged item was reiterated by the IFRS IC in March 2019 in a discussion within the context of the Application of the Highly Probable Requirement when a Specific Derivative is Designated as a Hedging Instrument (IFRS 9 and IAS 39).3 The IFRS IC discussed the application of the highly probable requirement with respect to a specific derivative (a load following swap) designated as a hedging instrument. In a load following swap the notional amount of the derivative designated as a hedging instrument varies depending on the outcome of the hedged item (e.g., forecast energy sales). The IFRS IC observed that forecast sales cannot be specified solely as a percentage of sales during a period because that would lack the required specificity in terms of magnitude and timing (see section 2.6.1 and section 6.3.3 below). It follows that for all hedge designations a known amount or component of hedged item must be identified. This would preclude any designations for which the designated amount or component of the hedged item is contingent, for example on the performance of market rate or indices. It follows that if the notional amount of the hedging derivative varies (e.g., a foreign currency forward contract with notional of CU100m plus an embedded knock in option to increase the notional to CU150m if a particular foreign currency rate is reached) that variability cannot be replicated in the designation of the hedged item.
IFRIC Update, March 2019, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting Even if the foreign currency forward contract with the embedded knock in option in the example was designated as hedging a known amount of hedged item it is unlikely to be an eligible hedge relationship. Consideration of the existence of an economic relationship and potentially whether the hedging instrument is a net written option could preclude hedge accounting (see sections 3.2.2, 3.6.2 and 6.4.1 below). An increasingly common example of contracts for which the notional amount may not be known in advance is long term contracts for renewable energy, often referred to as power purchase agreements. These could be either physical power purchase agreements (PPAs) or virtual power purchase agreements (VPPAs). In addition to the issues identified above in cases where the notional amount of the power purchase agreements is not known, achieving hedge accounting for these contracts can be very complex. In 2023 specific questions relating to power purchase agreements were submitted to the IFRS IC and escalated to the IASB. The IASB added a maintenance project to the work plan to explore whether narrow-scope amendments could be made to IFRS 9 to better reflect how financial statements are affected by purchase power agreements. On 8 May 2024, the IASB published the ED, Contracts for Renewable Electricity, Proposed amendments to IFRS 9 and IFRS 7. The ED contains proposed amendments to address how the ‘own-use’ requirements apply, permit hedge accounting if these contracts are used as hedging instruments, and disclosure requirements to enable investors to understand the effects of these contracts on a company’s financial performance and future cash flows (see section 3.2.6.A below).4 Nominal layer components are frequently used in risk management activities in practice. For hedge accounting purposes it is possible to designate a layer in a defined, but open, population (see examples (a) and (c) below), or from a defined nominal amount (see examples (b) and (d) below). An open population is one where the items within the population are not restricted to items that already exist. IFRS 9 includes the following examples of layers that could be eligible for hedge designation: [IFRS 9.B6.3.18] Extract from IFRS 9 B6.3.18 A layer component may be specified from a defined, but open, population, or from a defined nominal amount. Examples include: (a) part of a monetary transaction volume, for example, the next FC10 cash flows from sales denominated in a foreign currency after the first FC20 in March 201X;5 (b) a part of a physical volume, for example, the bottom layer, measuring 5 million cubic metres, of the natural gas stored in location XYZ; (c) a part of a physical or other transaction volume, for example, the first 100 barrels of the oil purchases in June 201X or the first 100 MWh of electricity sales in June 201X; or (d) a layer from the nominal amount of the hedged item, for example, the last CU80 million of a CU100 million firm commitment, the bottom layer of CU20 million of a CU100 million fixed-rate bond or the top layer of CU30 million from a total amount of CU100 million of fixed rate debt that can be prepaid at fair value (the defined nominal amount is CU100 million). In this Standard monetary amounts are denominated in ‘currency units’ (CU) and ‘foreign currency units’ (FC).
**[IFRS 9.B6.3.18].**
The ability to designate a ‘bottom layer’ nominal component for a group of forecast cash flows, such as the sale of the first 15,000 units of widgets, used in example (c) above, accommodates the fact that there could be a level of uncertainty as to the quantity of the hedged item. The bottom layer designation means that any uncertainty in forecast cash flows ‘above’ the bottom layer does not negatively impact the assessment as to whether the bottom layer itself is eligible as a hedged item (i.e., meets the highly probable requirement) (see section 2.6.1 below). However, it would not be possible to designate a ‘top layer’ nominal component for a group of forecast cash flows, as it is not possible to determine when that top layer occurs, as more forecast cash flows could always follow (see section 6.3.3 below). [IAS 39.F.3.10]. There are additional requirements if a layer is designated within a fair value hedge. An entity must specify it as a portion of a nominal amount, e.g., the top CHF 20 million layer of a CHF 100 million fixed rate liability. Accounting for a fair value hedge requires remeasurement of the hedged item for fair value changes attributable to the hedged risk (see section 7.1.1 below). In addition, that fair value hedge adjustment must be recognised in profit or loss no later than when the hedged item is derecognised (see section 7.1.2 below). Accordingly, it is necessary to track the designated hedged item so it can be determined when the hedged item has been derecognised. Applying this requirement to the example of a top CHF 20 million layer of a CHF 100 million fixed rate liability, the total defined nominal amount of CHF 100 million fixed rate
IFRS - Power Purchase Agreements available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting liability must be tracked in order to identify when the specified CHF 20 million top layer is derecognised [IFRS 9.B6.3.19]. If it is not possible to define a nominal amount in a way that facilitates tracking of the hedged item to which the fair value hedge adjustment relates, then hedge accounting would be precluded. Further analysis is also required on the ability to designate layers within a fair value hedge where there is prepayment risk (see section 2.3.2 below). 2.3.2 Layer component for fair value hedges with prepayment risk Although IFRS 9 allows the designation of layer components from a defined nominal amount as long as it is consistent with an entity’s risk management objective (see section 2.3.1 above), there are some additional considerations for fair value hedges where prepayment risk exists. [IFRS 9.B6.3.18, IFRS 9.B6.3.19]. A layer component that includes a prepayment option does not qualify as a hedged item in a fair value hedge if the fair value of the prepayment option is affected by changes in the hedged risk, unless the changes in fair value of the prepayment option as a result of changes in the hedged risk are included when measuring the change in fair value of the hedged item (see Illustration 2-10 below). [IFRS 9.B6.3.20]. However, if the hedged item includes a prepayment option such that it is prepayable at fair value, the fair value of the option is not affected by changes in the hedged risk, and hence it would be possible to designate a layer component in a fair value hedge with little or no ineffectiveness due to changes in the fair value of the option when measuring the hedged item (see Illustration 2-9 below). Illustration 2-9: Hedging a top layer of a loan prepayable at fair value An entity borrows money by issuing a CU10m five-year fixed rate loan. The entity has a prepayment option to pay back CU5m at fair value. The entity wants to be able to make use of the prepayment option without the amount repayable on early redemption being affected by interest rate changes. Consequently, the entity would like to hedge the fair value interest rate risk of the prepayable part of the loan. The fair value of the prepayment option is not affected by changes in the hedged risk as the layer is prepayable at fair value, hence it is possible to designate the top layer in a fair value hedge of interest rate risk. To achieve this, the entity enters into a five-year receive fixed/pay variable interest rate swap (IRS) with a notional amount of CU5m. The entity designates the IRS in a fair value hedge of the interest rate risk of the CU5m top layer of the loan attributable to the benchmark interest rate. As a result, the top layer is adjusted for changes in the fair value that are attributable to changes in the hedged risk. The bottom layer, which cannot be prepaid and is not hedged, remains at amortised cost. The gain or loss on the IRS will offset the change in fair value on the top layer attributable to the hedged risk. On prepayment, the fair value hedge adjustment of the top layer is part of the gain or loss from derecognition of a part of the loan as the result of the early repayment. The situation illustrated by Illustration 2-9 above, of a hedge of a top layer of a loan, would not often be found in practice as most prepayment options in loan agreements allow, in our experience, for prepayment at the nominal amount (instead of at fair value). Moreover, if a financial asset included an option that allowed prepayment at fair value, that would affect the assessment of the characteristics of the contractual cash flows. That assessment is a part of the classification of financial assets and such a prepayment option might not be consistent with payments that are solely principal and interest (see Chapter 44 at section 5.4.4). However, the ability to designate a top layer in a fair value hedge could be helpful when hedging a group of fixed rate readily transferable financial instruments, see section 2.5.2 below. As already mentioned above, IFRS 9 does not preclude fair value hedge accounting for layers including a prepayment option that are affected by changes in the hedged risk. However, in order to achieve hedge accounting for such a designation, changes in fair value of the prepayment option as a result of changes in the hedged risk have to be included in the assessment of whether the effectiveness requirements are met and when measuring the change in fair value of the hedged item.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting Illustration 2-10 illustrates what this means in practice: Illustration 2-10: Fair value hedge accounting designation using layers with and without prepayment risk A bank originates a CU10m five-year fixed rate loan with a prepayment option to pay back CU5m at any time at par. For risk management purposes, the loan is considered together with variable rate borrowings of CU10m. As a result, the bank is exposed to an interest margin risk resulting from the fix-to-floating interest rate mismatch. The bank expects the borrower to prepay CU2m and, therefore, wishes to hedge CU8m only. The bank enters into a five-year pay fixed/receive variable interest rate swap (IRS) with a notional amount of CU8m and designates CU5m of the IRS in a fair value hedge of the benchmark interest rate risk of the CU5m layer of the non-prepayable loan amount. In addition, the bank enters into a five year pay variable/receive fixed interest rate swaption with a notional amount of CU3m that is jointly designated with CU3m of the IRS to hedge the benchmark interest rate risk of the last remaining CU3m of the CU5m prepayable amount of the loan (a bottom layer of the prepayable portion of the loan). As a result, the CU5m non-prepayable loan amount is adjusted for changes in the fair value attributable to changes in the hedged risk (the fixed rate benchmark interest rate risk of a fixed term instrument). This is also true for the CU3m bottom layer of the prepayable amount, but in addition it must also be adjusted for the effect of changes in the fair value of the prepayment option that exists within the designated CU3m layer, attributable to changes in interest rate risk. The unhedged CU2m top layer remains at amortised cost. Therefore, the first CU2m of prepayments would have a gain or loss on derecognition determined as the difference between the amortised cost of the prepaid amount and par. For any further prepayments exceeding CU2m, the gain or loss on derecognition would be determined as the difference between the amortised cost including the fair value hedge adjustment and par. In this case the inclusion of fair value changes in the designated prepayable layer is unlikely to result in ineffectiveness as offset is provided by the CU3m swaption. However, in cases where the prepayment risk is not economically hedged by the hedging instrument, changes in fair value of the prepayment option will lead to ineffectiveness which has the potential to distort the economic relationship. This is discussed in more detail below in Illustration 2-12. When deciding on which instruments to transact in order to manage the interest rate risk in a prepayable portfolio, an entity will usually consider the likelihood of prepayment in a bottom layer. In particular it will consider whether the risk of prepayment is sufficient to justify the added expense of transacting a hedging instrument that can also be cancelled (e.g., an interest rate swap cancellable at zero cost or an offsetting swaption as described in Illustration 2-10 above). As part of these considerations, it is relatively common that from an economic perspective, an entity might view a bottom layer as having no prepayment risk attached at all and transact a non-cancellable hedging instrument accordingly. Despite this economic view, the hedge accounting guidance requires consideration of the fair value changes of that bottom layer based on the contractual terms of the hedged item, which would include the prepayment option (see section 7.4.12 below). Hence, for many economic hedges of bottom layers of prepayable hedged items, the changes in the fair values of the hedging instrument and the hedged item will not normally be the same, due to the unit of account required from an accounting perspective. This has the potential to distort the economic relationship between the pre-payable hedged item and the hedging instrument to the extent that hedge accounting might be precluded (see section 6.4.1 below). While it is unlikely that such a risk management objective would be undertaken unless there was an expectation that an economic relationship exists, the existence of the economic relationship would need to be demonstrated, perhaps via the use of a quantitative assessment. Additionally, as the expectation that an economic relationship exists could change over time, especially if the prepayment option looks likely to become in the money, or exercised for any other reason, this expectation would need to be periodically reassessed based on the updated facts and circumstances. Further, any actual ineffectiveness would be measured and recorded in profit or loss (see section 7.4 below).
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting ‘Bottom layer’ hedging strategies that avoid this source of ineffectiveness can only be applied if the hedged layer is not affected by the prepayment risk. This is best demonstrated by an example (see Illustration 2-11 below). Illustration 2-11: Hedging a bottom layer (no prepayment risk) of a loan portfolio A bank holds a portfolio of fixed rate loans with a total nominal amount of CU100m. The borrowers can, at any time during the tenor, prepay 20% of their (original) loan amount at par. For risk management purposes, the loans are considered together with variable rate borrowings of CU100m. As a result, the bank is exposed to an interest margin risk resulting from the fix-to-floating interest rate mismatch. The bank expects CU20m of loans to be prepaid. As part of the risk management strategy, the bank decides to hedge a part of the interest margin by entering into a pay fixed/receive variable interest rate swap (IRS). The objective is to hedge 95% of the amount of loans that is not prepayable using an IRS with a notional amount of CU76m (95% of CU80m). The hedged layer does not include a prepayment option. The IRS is designated in a fair value hedge of the interest rate risk of the CU76m bottom layer of the CU100m loan portfolio. As a result, the bottom layer is adjusted for changes in the fair value attributable to changes in the hedged risk (i.e., benchmark interest rate risk). The extent to which the borrowers exercise their prepayment option (i.e., up to 20% of the original loan) does not affect the hedging relationship. Also, if the bank were to derecognise any of the loans for any other reason, the first CU4m of non-prepayable amount of derecognised loans would not be part of the hedged item (i.e., the CU76m bottom layer). However, if the nominal amount of the loan fell below CU76m, this would start to affect the hedging relationship. Therefore, while IFRS 9 provides an effective solution for portfolios that feature a bottom layer that is not prepayable, as shown in Illustration 2-11 above, it does not provide an answer for portfolios that are fully prepayable at amount other than at fair value (for example, a residential fixed rate mortgage portfolio). The
*Reference: IASB decided to address hedging of such portfolios in its separate macro hedging project (see section 10*
below). Until that project is finalised, entities are allowed to apply the portfolio fair value hedging guidance in IAS 39. [IFRS 9.6.1.3]. Given the current lack of an effective hedge accounting solution within IFRS 9 for portfolios that feature a bottom layer that is prepayable, it is not uncommon for entities to consider alternative ‘proxy’ hedge accounting designations (see section 6.2.1 below). For example, an entity wishing to hedge an economic bottom layer within a group of items prepayable at par within a fair value hedge could identify specific items within the group (and designate those items only) or designate a percentage of the total as the hedged item. However, it is unlikely that an entity would choose to designate a hedge either way, as both these approaches are still likely to result in ineffectiveness. If specific items within the group were designated in a fair value hedge, and those specific items prepaid, then the designated hedged item would no longer exist, even if there was sufficient ‘non-designated’ items within the group to cover the amount designated. The issues arising from designation of a proportion are best explained with an example (see Illustration 2-12 below). Illustration 2-12: Hedging a proportion of a prepayable loan portfolio A bank holds a portfolio of fixed rate loans with a total nominal amount of CU100m. The borrowers can, at any time, prepay all of their (original) loan amount at par, plus an exit fee. The bank only expects 20% of the original loan amount to be prepaid before maturity. For risk management purposes, the loans are considered together with variable rate borrowings of CU100m. As a result, the bank is exposed to an interest margin risk resulting from the fix-to-floating interest rate mismatch. The bank expects CU20m of loans to be prepaid. As part of the risk management strategy, the bank decides to hedge the interest margin by entering into a pay fixed/receive variable interest rate swap (IRS). The objective is to hedge the amount of loans that they do not expect to prepay using an IRS with a notional amount of CU80m. The IRS is designated as a fair value hedge of 80% of the CU100m loan portfolio. After two years, loans of CU10m are prepaid, which is less than 20% and therefore does not affect the economic hedge in place. However, because of the proportionate designation, this is considered a reduction in the hedged amount for hedge accounting purposes. As a result, the entity now has an IRS of CU80m designated as a hedge of loans of CU72m ([CU100m - CU10m] × 80%), which will inevitably lead to some ineffectiveness. Another source of ineffectiveness will be fair value changes attributable to the embedded prepayment option within the loan portfolio, excluding changes in fair value of the embedded prepayment option that clearly arise from factors others than the change in hedged interest rate (see section 7.4.12 below).
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting A more common approach is for an entity hedging a bottom layer for risk management purposes is to use what is referred to as a proxy hedge, in which the hedged item might be floating rate liabilities and designate the derivatives as hedging them within a cash flow hedge (see section 6.2.1 below). This avoids the inclusion of fair value changes of the embedded prepayment option in the fixed rate loans as a source of potential ineffectiveness, but it does require the identification of sufficient suitable floating rate liabilities. 2.4 The ‘sub-benchmark rate issue’ Some financial institutions are able to raise funding at interest rates that are below a benchmark interest rate (e.g., benchmark interest rate minus 15 basis points (bp)). In such a scenario, the entity might wish to remove the variability in future cash flows caused by movements in the benchmark interest rate. IFRS 9 does not allow the designation of a ‘full’ benchmark risk component (i.e., benchmark rate flat) in this situation, as a component cannot be more than the total cash flows of the entire item. Although historically this is often referred to as the ‘sub-LIBOR issue’ it is not specific to LIBOR. It will remain an issue for any funding raised below a benchmark rate even now the global Interest Rate Benchmark Reform forced markets to move away from LIBOR as the predominant benchmark interest rate (see the Supplement: IBOR Reform). However, as the risk-free rates that will replace LIBOR include a much smaller credit spread, the issue will become much less common in future.
**[IFRS 9.B6.3.21, IFRS 9.B6.3.22].**
Part of the reason for this restriction is that a contractual interest rate is often ‘floored’ at zero, so that interest will never become negative. The existence of floors can result in accounting and economic outcomes that are inconsistent with hedge accounting. For example, Entity B issues debt at a benchmark rate minus 15bp, but the interest payable is floored at zero. Entity B also enters into a benchmark rate hedging swap with the aim of removing the variability in future cash flows with respect to the benchmark rate, locking in an interest margin. Unless the hedging swap similarly has a floor, then if the benchmark rate decreases below 15bp this would cause cash flow variability for the hedging swap but not for the hedged item. Permitting the designation of a benchmark rate risk component in the hedged item and ignoring the effect of the floor on the variability of the interest cash flows would inappropriately defer hedge ineffectiveness (see section 7.4 below). Consequently, any designated component has to be less than or equal to the cash flows of the entire item. [IFRS 9.B6.3.21,
**IFRS 9.BC6.225, IFRS 9.BC6.226, IFRS 9.BC6.227, IFRS 9.BC6.228].**
In this scenario, the entity could instead designate, as the hedged item, the variability in cash flows of the entire liability (or a proportion of it), but only for changes attributable to the benchmark rate. [IFRS 9.B6.3.24]. Such a designation excludes any ineffectiveness from changes in the credit risk specific to the borrower, as these are excluded from the hedge relationship, however it will not be perfectly effective. Ineffectiveness could arise as follows in the hedge designation described above: • For financial instruments that have an interest rate floor of zero in situations in which the forward curve for a part of the remaining hedged term is below 15 basis points. This is because the hedged item will have less variability in cash flows as a result of interest rate changes than a swap without such a floor. In fact, an expectation that the benchmark rate will consistently be below 15 basis points could cause the hedge relationship to fail the effectiveness criteria as there is no economic relationship between the hedged item and hedging instrument. This would preclude hedge accounting entirely (see section 6.4.1 below). However, if the benchmark rate is above 15 basis points and is not expected to go below it, this source of ineffectiveness would not be expected to arise. The hedged item will have the same cash flow variability as a liability without a floor. • Ineffectiveness is also likely to occur for example from changes in the time value of the embedded floor in the hedged item, as no offset will arise from the fair value changes of a swap without a floor (see section 7.4.12 below). • Even if the variability of cash flows in the hedged item and hedging instrument might be the same, the absolute cash flows are not, due to the inclusion of the negative spread in the hedged item. Accordingly, the discounted cash flows will not perfectly offset (see section 7.4 below). • The discount curve used to discount the hedged cash flows needs to be based on a benchmark rate (see section 7.4.5 below). However, there is a choice between discounting the hedged cash flows at the benchmark rate or at the benchmark rate minus the negative credit spread (15 basis points in the example above), as the guidance is not prescriptive. The negative credit spread would not be updated for changes in the borrower’s credit risk as that risk is excluded from the hedge relationship. This is consistent with the general hedge accounting approach of calculating a change in the present value of the hedged item with respect to the hedged risk and not a full fair value. The second option for discounting could improve effectiveness but some ongoing ineffectiveness is likely to remain (see section 7.4 below).
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting The prohibition on identifying a benchmark rate risk component in a variable instrument priced at a sub- benchmark rate (i.e., a benchmark rate minus a spread) as outlined above, has also some relevance for fixed rate instruments. Let us consider a fixed rate item that is originally priced based on a benchmark rate minus a spread, for example, a fixed-rate financial liability for which the contractual interest rate is initially fixed at 100 basis points below the benchmark rate. It would not be possible to identify the hedged item as having fixed interest cash flows equal to the benchmark, however an entity can designate as the hedged item the entire liability (i.e., principal plus interest equal to the benchmark rate minus 100 basis points) for changes in value attributable to changes in the benchmark rate. [IFRS 9.B6.3.23]. The negative interest rate environment in some countries has further implications on the designation of risk components in connection with the sub-benchmark issue (see section 2.4.2 below). While the example in the standard uses LIBOR as the benchmark rate component, it is clear that the requirement relates not just to financial items in general, but is a general prohibition on the cash flows of hedged risk components being larger than the cash flows of the entire hedged item. This means that the sub-LIBOR issue is also applicable to non-financial items where the contract price is linked to a benchmark price minus a differential. This is best demonstrated using an example derived from the application guidance of IFRS 9 (see Illustration 2-13 below).
Illustration 2-13: Sub-benchmark rate issue - selling crude oil at below benchmark price An entity has a long-term sales contract to sell crude oil of a specific quality to a specified location. The contract includes a clause that sets the price per barrel at West Texas Intermediate (WTI) minus USD10 with a minimum price of USD30. The entity wishes to hedge the WTI benchmark price risk by entering into a WTI future. As outlined above, the entity cannot designate a ‘full’ WTI component, i.e., a WTI component that ignores the price differential and the minimum price. However, the entity could designate the WTI future as a hedge of the entire cash flow variability under the sales contract that is attributable to the change in the benchmark price. When doing so, the hedged item would have the same cash flow variability as a sale of crude oil at the WTI price (or above), as long as the forward price for the remaining hedged term does not fall below USD40. [IFRS 9.B6.3.25]. Where the hedged item has the same cash flow variability as the hedging instrument there could be an expectation that the hedge relationship will meet the effectiveness criteria. However, any ineffectiveness needs to be measured and recorded in profit or loss (see section 7.4 below). In some cases, the contract price might not be defined as a benchmark price minus a fixed differential but as a benchmark price plus a pricing differential (the ‘basis spread’) that is sometimes positive and sometimes negative. The market structure could reveal that items are priced that way and there might even be derivatives available for the basis spread (i.e., basis swaps, for example the benchmark gas oil crack spread derivative which is a derivative for the price differential between crude oil and gas oil which reflects the refining margin [IFRS 9.B6.3.10(c)(i)]). Similar to Illustration 2-13 above, an entity could not designate the benchmark price component as the hedged item given that the cash flows of the benchmark price component could be more than the total cash flows of the entire item. Unfortunately, the standard does not provide any guidance as to how an entity might assess whether the basis spread could be negative or not. For example, it is not clear whether an entity is only required to look at the forward benchmark prices or whether it is required to consider all reasonably possible scenarios. The use of ‘reasonably possible scenarios’ in other places in IFRS 9 might indicate the latter. A related question is whether the entity could designate the entire cash flow variability that is attributable to changes in only the benchmark price risk (as the entity alternatively does as shown in Illustration 2-13 above) if that risk is a non-contractually specified risk component. This assessment is likely to be similar to whether a non-contractually specified risk component is separately identifiable and reliably measurable (as outlined in section 2.2.3 above), which would be required in order to determine the variability of the entire cash flows with respect to changes in the benchmark price. However, the presence of a spread that is sometimes negative could make this assessment more difficult. An entity needs to prove that the benchmark price cash flows plus or minus the spread make up the total cash flows. This might be the case, for example, if it can be proven that there are quality differences, such that the benchmark price is sometimes of better quality and sometimes worse than the hedged exposure. On the other hand, if the basis spread switches between positive and negative because of individual supply and demand drivers of either the benchmark price or the price of the hedged exposure, this could indicate that the benchmark price is not implicit in the fair value or cash flows of the hedged exposure. To illustrate this with an example, we could take WTI and Brent crude oil prices. While both prices might be highly correlated, WTI could not be identified as a benchmark price component in Brent because both prices have their own supply and demand drivers. Furthermore, it would not be possible to
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting determine whether either WTI is a risk component of Brent or vice versa, which demonstrates that there is no risk component that can be designated in a hedge relationship. In that case, hedge accounting would only be permitted if the full cash flows were designated for all changes in the contract price, and assuming the other eligibility requirements are met. 2.4.1 Late hedges of benchmark rate portions of fixed rate instruments There is no requirement in IFRS 9 for hedge accounting to be designated on initial recognition of either the hedged item or the hedging instrument. [IFRS 9.B6.5.28]. In particular, it is not uncommon for risk management and/or hedge accounting to be applied sometime after initial recognition of the hedged item. This is often referred to as a ‘late hedge’. There is no additional guidance in IFRS 9 on how to apply hedge accounting to ‘late hedges’ over and above the usual criteria. However, the identification of eligible risk components in fixed rate hedged items in late hedges can be problematic. An example of the difficulties of such a designation is discussed in the application guidance to IFRS 9 with respect to a fixed rate financial instrument that is hedged sometime after its origination using an on market derivative, and interest rates have changed in the meantime, such that the fixed rate on the instrument is now below the benchmark rate i.e., the fixed rate in the hedging derivative. Despite the requirement for risk components to be less than or equal to the total cash flows of the entire item, in this case it could be possible for the entity to designate a risk component equal to the benchmark rate that is higher than the contractual rate paid on the item. This is provided that the benchmark rate is still less than the effective interest rate (yield) calculated as if the instrument had been purchased on the day it was first designated as the hedged item. [IFRS 9.B6.3.21, IFRS 9.B6.3.23]. This is illustrated below. Illustration 2-14: Hedge of a portion of an existing fixed rate financial asset following a rise in interest rates Company B originates a fixed rate financial asset of CU100 that has an effective interest rate of 6% at a time when the benchmark rate is 4%. B begins to hedge that asset sometime later using an on market derivative when the benchmark rate has increased to 8% and the fair value of the asset has decreased to CU90 (reflecting an effective yield of 9.5%). The asset matures in four years’ time. The future annual cash flows on the financial asset are given below.
Future cash flows on existing fixed rate financial asset CU6 CU6 CU6 CU106 Because the current benchmark rate of 8% is less than this current effective yield of 9.5%, the entity can designate a benchmark rate component of 8% as a risk component of the 9.5% effective yield. If the entity does designate a benchmark rate component of 8%, the hedged cash flows will consist partly of the contractual interest cash flows and partly of the difference between the net present value discounted at the hedged risk of 8% (CU93) and the amount repayable on maturity (CU100). [IFRS 9.B6.3.23]. This is further demonstrated in the table below. Effective Yield Asset calculation basis Asset value Original EIR at 6% Amortised cost CU100 Current effective yield at 9.5% Current fair value CU90 Hedged risk at 8% Net present value of the hedged item discounted at 8% CU93
The guidance illustrated in Illustration 2-14 above will assist entities in designating hedges in a way that significantly reduces ineffectiveness. However, some ineffectiveness is still likely to arise if the hedging instrument has a fixed rate that reflects the 8% benchmark rate. This is because the timing of the 8% cash flows from the hedging instrument (i.e., an interest rate swap with a fixed leg at 8%) is unlikely to match fully the timing of the hedged cash flows, which are represented by the actual future contractual coupons (e.g., 6%) and an amount of principal payable on maturity. It is also not uncommon for an existing derivative to be designated as a hedging instrument of new or existing fixed rate hedged items. It is likely that the existing hedging instrument will have a non-zero fair value on designation and will have a fixed rate that is different to the prevailing benchmark rate for the remaining term of the hedged item.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting The ability to designate a risk component and/or a nominal amount of the fixed rate hedged item can be helpful in achieving fair value hedge accounting. [IFRS 9.6.3.7]. This is illustrated below. Illustration 2-15: Hedge of a portion of a fixed rate financial liability with an existing derivative with a non-zero fair value Company C issues new debt of CU200m fixed rate debt at par, the pricing of which was determined based on the prevailing benchmark rate of 1.5%, plus a credit spread of 200bp resulting in a fixed coupon of 3.5%. Company C identifies an existing CU200m receive fixed 2%, pay floating interest rate swap with the same residual maturity as the issued debt. The existing swap enables Company C to reduce the variability in the fair value of the issued debt with respect to the benchmark rate, consistent with their risk management. The benchmark rate is a separately identifiable and reliably measurable risk component of the fixed rate debt, considering the market structure within which it was issued (see section 2.2.3.A above). Hence it is possible for Company C to designate the fair value hedge with respect only to changes in the benchmark rate. It is also possible for Company C to identify a nominal component of the fixed rate debt, i.e., a specified part of the fixed rate debt (see section 2.3.1 above). [IFRS 9.6.3.7(c)]. In that case Company C could choose to designate the hedged item to be the full CU200m principal of the fixed rate debt, but only an amount equivalent to a 2% coupon of each actual fixed rate 3.5% interest coupon, for changes in fair value with respect to the benchmark rate. Such a strategy has the benefit of improving offset of fair value changes in both the hedged item and the hedging instrument due to the benchmark rate, as both have a fixed rate of 2%. This could result in lower levels of ineffectiveness. However, as fair value hedge accounting for the hedged item only reflects the cumulative fair value changes with respect to the hedged risk since designation, care would need to be taken to ensure an appropriate amortisation approach was applied such that the fair value hedge adjustment is fully amortised by maturity of the hedge relationship (see section 7.1.2 below). This approach is only possible because the fixed rate on the interest rate swap (i.e., 2%) is less than the actual coupon on the fixed rate debt (i.e., 3.5%). If the swap rate was higher (e.g., 4%) then it would not be possible to designate the full CU200m principal of the fixed rate debt plus a 4% coupon as the hedged item, as any designated component has to be less than or equal to the cash flows of the entire item. [IFRS 9.B6.3.21]. Company C could instead choose to designate the hedged item to be the full CU200m principal of the fixed rate debt but with an amount equivalent to a 1.5% coupon (i.e., the prevailing benchmark rate) for changes in fair value with respect to the benchmark rate. In that case some ineffectiveness would arise but the amortisation of the fair value hedge adjustment over the life of the hedge relationship is simpler. This designation strategy would not work if hedging a floating rate instrument with an existing derivative with a non-zero fair value as it is not possible to identify an off-market risk component in a cash flow hedge to match an off-market hedging instrument (see section 7.4.3 below). 2.4.2 Negative interest rates Some countries have, or have had negative interest rate environments, for example Switzerland, Japan and certain countries in the Eurozone. The requirements in consideration of whether a benchmark rate is an eligible risk component in a financial instrument are the same irrespective of whether the benchmark rate is positive or negative (see sections 2.2.2 and 2.2.3.A above). The following Illustration shows this for a fair value hedge. Illustration 2-16: Negative interest rates and fair value hedges Assume the following scenarios:
(a) Bank A enters into a €1 million loan to a corporate at a fixed coupon of 3.5%. The coupon has been
determined considering the negative Euro Short-Term Rate (€STR) rate (the benchmark rate) of minus 0.15% plus a credit spread of 3.65%.
(b) Bank B acquires debt securities in the secondary market, issued by a highly rated corporate entity, with a
€1 million notional and fixed coupon of 3.5%. In this fact pattern the debt was issued some years ago when benchmark interest rates were much higher. The purchase price of the debt is €1.185 million which results in an effective interest rate of minus 0.18%, consisting of the negative €STR rate (the benchmark rate) when the debt is acquired of minus 0.15% and a credit spread of minus 0.03%. Both Bank A and Bank B want to hedge the fixed rate benchmark rate component and each enter into an interest rate swap with a €1 million principal, paying fixed minus 0.15% and receiving €STR. The banks wish to designate the benchmark rate component in a fair value hedge for changes in €STR.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting In scenario a) it seems acceptable for the bank to designate the benchmark rate risk component because: • It is included in the pricing of the hedged item and is thought to meet the separately identifiable and reliably measurable criteria (see section 2.2.3.A above). • The benchmark rate can be positive or negative, therefore the cash flows representing that benchmark rate can also be positive or negative, even if they are part of overall positive cash flows (which is similar to a benchmark rate component of 4% hedging the benchmark rate risk in a coupon of 5%, except that the benchmark rate is negative in this case). And • The benchmark rate cash flows are less than the cash flows in the hedged items (i.e., minus 0.15% which is less than 3.5%). We would find it difficult to reach the same conclusion for scenario b) as the benchmark rate is higher than the effective interest rate (i.e., minus 0.15% which is greater than minus 0.18%). [IFRS 9.B6.3.23]. This is consistent with the fact that if a debt instrument were to be issued at par bearing a coupon of minus 0.18%, which included a credit spread of minus 0.03% when the benchmark rate was minus 0.15%, it would not be possible to identify a benchmark rate component for hedge accounting purposes. [IFRS 9.B6.3.21]. In both scenarios, the banks would not be permitted to designate a payment of minus 0.15% of the principal as an eligible component of a receipt of a 3.5% coupon. [IFRS 9.6.3.7(b)]. This is because it is difficult to argue that a payment of a negative 0.15% of the principal is a portion of a receipt of 3.5% of that principal. However, this is not that relevant in scenario a) as it would be possible to designate a separately identifiable benchmark rate component in the total cash flows, as noted above. Notwithstanding the above, in both cases, the banks can designate all the cash flows in the financial asset for changes in the benchmark rate, although this is likely to result in some ineffectiveness. [IFRS 9.B6.3.21]. Illustration 2-16 above relates to fair value hedges of fixed rate instruments. When applying cash flow hedge accounting of interest rate risk in a negative interest rate environment, further complications arise if an instrument (typically the hedged item) is floored at zero. If there is an ‘in the money floor’ in the hedged item, such that no interest is currently payable or expected to be payable in the future, then there is no cash flow variability with respect to the hedged risk, hence cash flow hedge accounting is precluded (see sections 5 and 5.2 below). [IFRS 9.6.5.2]. Further, in a negative interest rate environment if the hedged item contains a floor at zero, and no such floor exists in the hedging instrument, even if there was an expectation that interest rates might become positive again within the term of the hedge relationship, demonstrating that an economic relationship exists between the hedged item and hedging instrument could be tricky (see section 6.4.1 below), in which case hedge accounting would cease. [IFRS 9.6.4.1(c)(i)]. This is because until interest rate become positive again there is no variability in the hedged cash flows from changes in interest rates (i.e., they are fixed at zero), but cash flows on the floating leg of the hedging instrument will reflect all changes in the interest rate. In fact, such a strategy would result in increased cash flow variability as a result of the risk management activities. The issues arising from the existence of floors within a negative interest rate environment are similar to those within sub-benchmark rate instruments (see section 2.4 above).
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting 2.5 Groups of items 2.5.1 General requirements Extract from IFRS 9 6.6.1 A group of items (including a group of items that constitute a net position; see paragraphs B6.6.1-B6.6.8) is an eligible hedged item only if: (a) it consists of items (including components of items) that are, individually, eligible hedged items; (b) the items in the group are managed together on a group basis for risk management purposes; and (c) in the case of a cash flow hedge of a group of items whose variabilities in cash flows are not expected to be approximately proportional to the overall variability in cash flows of the group so that offsetting risk positions arise: (i) it is a hedge of foreign currency risk; and (ii) the designation of that net position specifies the reporting period in which the forecast transactions are expected to affect profit or loss, as well as their nature and volume (see paragraphs B6.6.7-B6.6.8).
**[IFRS 9.6.6.1]**
Illustration 2-17: Hedging a portfolio of shares An entity holds a portfolio of shares of Swiss companies that replicates the Swiss Market Index (SMI). The entity elected to account for the shares at fair value through other comprehensive income without subsequent reclassification to profit or loss, as allowed by IFRS 9 (see Chapter 44 at section 7). The entity decides to lock in the current value of the portfolio by entering into corresponding SMI futures contracts. The individual shares would be eligible hedged items if hedged individually (using derivatives on the price of each share). As the objective of the portfolio is to replicate the SMI, the entity can also demonstrate that the shares are managed together on a group basis. The entity also assesses the compliance with the criteria for hedge accounting (see section 6 below). Consequently, the entity designates the SMI futures contracts as the hedging instrument in a hedge of the fair value of the portfolio. As a result, the gains or losses on the SMI futures are accounted for in OCI (without subsequent reclassification to profit or loss) in the same manner as the shares, thus eliminating the accounting mismatch (see section 2.6.3 below). [IFRS 9.6.5.3]. Whether the items in the group are managed together on a group basis is a matter of fact, i.e., it depends on an entity’s behaviour and cannot be achieved by mere documentation. The IFRS 9 eligibly criteria for groups of items for hedge accounting also permits designation of net positions if an entity hedges on a net basis for risk management purposes. [IFRS 9.BC6.435]. Whether an entity hedges in this way is a matter of fact (not merely of assertion or documentation). Hence an entity cannot apply hedge accounting on a net basis solely to achieve a particular accounting outcome if that would not reflect its risk management approach. [IFRS 9.6.6.1(b), IFRS 9.B6.6.1]. In addition, there are some further restrictions for cash flow hedges of net positions (see section 2.5.3 below). [IFRS 9.BC6.436]. IFRS 9 also contains special presentation requirements when hedging net positions, which are discussed in sections 2.5.3 and 9.3 below.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting 2.5.2 Hedging a component of a group A group designation can also consist of a component of a group of items, such as a layer component of a group. A component could also be a proportion of a group of items, such as 50% of a fixed rate bond series with a total volume of CU100 million. Whether an entity designates a layer component or a proportionate component depends on the entity’s risk management objective. [IFRS 9.6.6.1, IFRS 9.6.6.2]. Extract from IFRS 9 6.6.3 A layer component of an overall group of items (for example, a bottom layer) is eligible for hedge accounting only if: (a) it is separately identifiable and reliably measurable; (b) the risk management objective is to hedge a layer component; (c) the items in the overall group from which the layer is identified are exposed to the same hedged risk (so that the measurement of the hedged layer is not significantly affected by which particular items from the overall group form part of the hedged layer); (d) for a hedge of existing items (for example, an unrecognised firm commitment or a recognised asset) an entity can identify and track the overall group of items from which the hedged layer is defined (so that the entity is able to comply with the requirements for the accounting for qualifying hedging relationships); and (e) any items in the group that contain prepayment options meet the requirements for components of a nominal amount (see paragraph B6.3.20).
**[IFRS 9.6.6.3].**
The benefits of identifying a layer component, as discussed in section 2.3 above, are relevant when applied to a group of items. In fact, the bottom layer hedging strategy discussed in Illustration 2-11 in section 2.3.2 above is, in fact, a designation of a component of a group. The following Illustration provides a different example of designating a component of a group, this time a top layer. Illustration 2-18: Hedging a top layer of a portfolio of financial liabilities A bank completes a bond issuance of CU50 million that is made up of 50,000 fixed rate bonds with a face value of CU1,000 each. The bank expects that it will repurchase up to CU10 million of the issue before maturity. Therefore, it chooses to transact a receive fixed/pay variable interest rate swap with a notional amount of CU10 million, in order to hedge the benchmark rate component of the fair value interest rate risk of the volume that is expected to be repurchased. From an economic perspective, that hedge would allow repurchases of up to CU10 million total face value for which the gain or loss from changes in the benchmark rate would be compensated by the gain or loss on the swap. However, this can only be reflected in the accounting if the entity designates a CU10 million top layer. The entity would revalue the first CU10 million of face value of the bond issue expected to be repurchased for changes in the benchmark rate risk, and would include a fair value hedge gain or loss when determining the gain or loss on derecognition of the first CU10 million of bonds when they are repurchased. If it was not permitted to designate a layer of a group of items, entities would in such cases either have to identify individual items within the group and designate them on a standalone basis, or prorate the fair value hedge gain or loss to the entire bond issue volume, as discussed in section 2.3.2 above. The IASB believes this would result in arbitrary accounting results and decided to allow a layer component designation for a group of items. [IFRS 9.BC6.438, IFRS 9.BC6.439]. Designation of a layer component which includes a prepayment option is discussed in section 2.3.2 above. Based on the information provided, the top layer in Illustration 2-18 above would meet the criteria in order for a top layer to be designated. Extract from IFRS 9 B6.6.12 A hedging relationship can include layers from several different groups of items. For example, in a hedge of a net position of a group of assets and a group of liabilities, the hedging relationship can comprise, in combination, a layer component of the group of assets and a layer component of the group of liabilities.
**[IFRS 9.B6.6.12].**
The hedge of a net position of a group of assets and a group of liabilities is discussed in section 2.5.1 above and section 2.5.3 below.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting 2.5.3 Cash flow hedge of a net position Many entities are exposed to foreign currency risk arising from purchases and sales of goods or services denominated in foreign currencies. Cash inflows and outflows occurring on forecast transactions in the same foreign currency are often economically hedged on a net basis, as illustrated in the below example. Illustration 2-19: Economic ‘natural hedge’ of foreign currency cash flows An entity with GBP as its functional currency anticipates foreign currency denominated sales of USD100 in 12 months and also intends to purchase fixed assets of USD80 in 12 months. USD80 of the cash inflows of the forecast sales are economically hedged together with the cash outflows for the forecast purchase of the fixed assets. A single foreign currency forward contract to sell USD20 in 12 months is transacted to hedge the net position arising from the sales and purchases. In the case of a cash flow hedge of a group of items whose variabilities in cash flows are not expected to be approximately proportional to the overall variability in cash flows of the group so that offsetting or net risk positions arise, designation is only permitted for a hedge of foreign currency risk. Said differently, designation of a net position in a cash flow hedge is limited to hedges of foreign currency risk under IFRS 9. [IFRS 9.6.6.1(c), IFRS 9.B6.6.7, IFRS 9.BC6.455]. The standard mechanics of cash flow hedge accounting cannot accommodate a hedged net position whose gross cash flows affect profit or loss in different periods (see section 7.2 below). Applying standard cash flow hedge accounting to Illustration 2-19 above, the gain or loss accumulated in other comprehensive income (OCI) on the USD20 of hedging instrument would be reclassified to profit or loss when the revenue transaction occurs. However, this will only offset the gain or loss on USD20 of the USD100 hedged revenue while the remaining revenue of FC80 and the fixed asset purchase of USD80 (i.e., the economic hedge) would still be measured at the spot rate. This would result in the bottom line profit for the period(s) not reflecting the economic hedge. IFRS 9 amends the standard cash flow hedge accounting for such a net position in that the foreign currency gain or loss on the USD80 revenue cash flows that affect profit or loss in the earlier period must be carried forward to offset the foreign currency gain or loss on the fixed asset purchase cash flows that will affect profit or loss in later periods. This is achieved by deferring the gain or loss on the natural hedge in OCI, with a reclassification to profit or loss once the offsetting cash flows affect profit or loss (see section 9.3.1 and Illustration 2-20 below). However, the gross transactions that make up the net position are recognised when they arise and will be measured at the spot foreign currency rate ruling at that time. They are not adjusted to reflect the result of the hedge. The whole impact of hedge accounting must be presented in a separate line item in profit or loss. [IFRS 9.6.6.4]. This separate line item includes: • The reclassification adjustment for gains or losses on the hedge of the net position • The gain or loss on the natural hedge, with the counter-entry being recognised in OCI And • The later reclassification adjustment of the gain or loss on the natural hedge from OCI to profit or loss.
**[IFRS 9.B6.6.15].**
The rather complicated accounting described above is best illustrated using an example: Illustration 2-20: Cash flow hedge of a foreign currency net position An entity having CAD as functional currency anticipates sales of GBP100m in 12 months and also plans a major capital expenditure (fixed assets) of GBP80m in 12 months. The anticipated sales and capital expenditure (i.e., the group of forecast transactions) are designated as the hedged item and the resulting net position is hedged with a forward contract to sell GBP20m in 12 months. The fixed assets will be depreciated on a straight-line basis over eight years. For simplicity, assume the spot rate equals the forward rate. GBP/CAD spot rate At inception of the hedge (beginning of year 1) 1.50 After 12 months (end of year 1) 1.60

To defer the natural hedge gain from profit or loss to OCI (GBP80 × [1.60 - 1.50]). The net profit for the period is CAD 150, which represents the sale of GBP100 at the hedged rate of 1.50 (albeit presented in two different line items).

Financial instruments: Hedge accounting
Statement of financial position (CAD) Y1 Y2 Y3 Y4 Y5 Y6 Y7 Y8 Y9 Cash Property, plant and equipment - Hedging reserve (OCI) (8) (7) (6) (5) (4) (3) (2) (1) - The transactions within a net position still must be measured at their spot rates, while the effect of the hedge is presented in a separate line item. [IFRS 9.B6.6.15]. In other words, although an entity could be economically hedged from a bottom line (or net) perspective, volatility will still arise in the amounts reported for the individual hedged transactions (on a gross basis), and it is only the bottom line of profit or loss that will reflect the benefits of the hedge. For a net position to qualify for cash flow hedge accounting the hedge documentation has to include, for each type of item within the net position, its amount and nature as well as the reporting period in which it is expected to affect profit or loss. This is expected to be a relatively detailed record of what makes up the net position and how it will impact profit or loss, including the depreciation profile, if relevant. [IFRS 9.6.6.1(c)(ii),
**IFRS 9.B6.6.7, IFRS 9.B6.6.8].**
2.5.4 Nil net positions IFRS 9 also addresses hedges of nil net positions. This refers to when entities hedge a group of items where the hedged items themselves fully offset the risk that is managed. An example would be similar to the scenario illustrated by Illustration 2-19 above but where the entity anticipates sales of GBP100m in 12 months and also plans a major capital expenditure of GBP100m in 12 months. Hedge accounting for a net nil position is possible, applying the same treatment as for hedges of net positions in section 2.5.1 and section 2.5.3 above, however there are some additional requirements that must be met. Extract from IFRS 9 6.6.6 When the hedged item is a group that is a nil net position (ie the hedged items among themselves fully offset the risk that is managed on a group basis), an entity is permitted to designate it in a hedging relationship that does not include a hedging instrument, provided that: (a) the hedge is part of a rolling net risk hedging strategy, whereby the entity routinely hedges new positions of the same type as time moves on (for example, when transactions move into the time horizon for which the entity hedges); (b) the hedged net position changes in size over the life of the rolling net risk hedging strategy and the entity uses eligible hedging instruments to hedge the net risk (ie when the net position is not nil); (c) hedge accounting is normally applied to such net positions when the net position is not nil and it is hedged with eligible hedging instruments; and (d) not applying hedge accounting to the nil net position would give rise to inconsistent accounting outcomes, because the accounting would not recognise the offsetting risk positions that would otherwise be recognised in a hedge of a net position.
**[IFRS 9.6.6.6].**
2.6 Other eligibility issues for hedged items 2.6.1 Highly probable Forecast transactions (or a component thereof) can only be eligible hedged items if they are highly probable of occurring (see section 2.1 above). [IFRS 9.6.3.3]. The term ‘highly probable’ is not defined in IFRS 9 but is often interpreted to have a much greater likelihood of happening than ‘more likely than not’. The Implementation Guidance within IAS 39 contained some guidance as to how the term highly probable is applied within the context of hedge accounting and, this guidance can be considered relevant for IFRS 9 hedge accounting. The IAS 39 guidance indicates that an assessment of the likelihood that a forecast transaction will take place is not based solely on management’s intention, it should be supported by
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting observable facts and the attendant circumstances. In such an assessment, an entity should consider the following: • The frequency of similar past transactions • The financial and operational ability of the entity to carry out the transaction • Whether substantial commitments of resources have been made to a particular activity • The extent of loss or disruption of operations that could result if the transaction does not occur • The likelihood that transactions with substantially different characteristics might be used to achieve the same business purpose And • The entity’s business plan [IAS 39.F.3.7] Although, by the nature of a forecasted transaction, the terms and timing of the transaction may not be known with certainty, if the key terms cannot be estimated with reasonable specificity then it may be difficult to determine that the forecasted transaction is indeed highly probable. [IAS 39.F.3.11]. Reasonable specificity in terms of a identifying a forecast transaction is necessary both in order to measure any ineffectiveness from the hedge relationship (see section 7.4 below) and also to determine whether and when the documented hedged item occurs (see section 8.3 below). In March 2019, the IFRS IC discussed the application of the highly probable requirement with respect to a specific derivative (a load following swap) designated as a hedging instrument.5 In a load following swap the notional amount of the derivative designated as a hedging instrument varies depending on the outcome of the hedged item (e.g., forecast energy sales). As part of that specific discussion, the IFRS IC observed more generally that the terms of the hedging instrument do not affect the highly probable assessment because the highly probable requirement is applicable to the hedged item (see section 6.3.3 below). For example, an entity that wishes to hedge the foreign currency risk of a future business combination, can only achieve hedge accounting once the business combination itself is highly probable (see section 2.6.4 below). This is the case even if the hedging instrument is a deal contingent foreign currency contract, such that if the business combination does not go ahead, the foreign currency contract is terminated with no cash settlement. In addition, an entity might also need to consider the impact of wider industry and business drivers as to whether cash flows are highly probable or not. For example: • Whether forecast cash flows in a subsidiary that is due to be sold can be considered highly probable in the parent’s consolidated financial statements (see section 7.2.4 below) Or • If a designated benchmark interest rate is no longer expected to exist, whether forecast cash flows linked to that benchmark rate can be highly probable (see the Supplement: IBOR Reform) The length of time until a forecast transaction is projected to occur is also a factor. Other factors being equal, the more distant a forecast transaction is, the less likely it is that it would be considered highly probable and the stronger the evidence required to determine that it is highly probable. For example, a transaction forecast to occur in 5 years will often be less likely to occur than a transaction forecast to occur in one year. However, forecast interest payments for the next 20 years on variable rate debt would typically be highly probable if supported by an existing contractual obligation. An entity might also need to consider the credit risk associated with the counterparty to a forecast transaction. Even though a forecast transaction does not involve credit risk, depending on the possible counterparties for the anticipated transaction, the credit risk that affects them can indirectly affect the assessment of whether the forecast transaction is highly probable or not (see section 6.4.2.B below). In addition, other factors being equal, the greater the physical quantity or future value of the forecast transaction, in proportion to the entity’s total transactions of the same nature, the less likely it is that the transaction would be considered highly probable. For example, less evidence generally would be needed to support forecast highly probable sales of 100,000 units in the next month than 950,000 units in that month, when recent sales have averaged 950,000 for the past 3 months. A history of having designated hedges of forecast transactions and then determining that they are no longer expected to occur would call into question both an entity’s ability to predict forecast transactions accurately and the propriety of using hedge accounting in the future for similar transactions. [IAS 39.F.3.7]. However, the standard contains no prescriptive ‘tainting’ provisions in this area. Therefore, entities are not
IFRIC Update, March 2019, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting automatically prohibited from using cash flow hedge accounting if a forecast transaction fails to occur. Instead, whenever such a situation arises the particular facts, circumstances and evidence must be assessed to determine whether doubt has, in fact, been cast on an entity’s ongoing hedging strategies. It is also explained in the IAS 39 Implementation Guidance that cash flows arising after the prepayment date on an instrument that is prepayable at the issuer’s option could be highly probable for a group or pool of similar assets for which prepayments can be estimated with a high degree of accuracy, e.g., mortgage loans, or if the prepayment option is significantly out of the money. In addition, the cash flows after the prepayment date may be designated as the hedged item if a comparable option exists in the hedging instrument (see section 7.4.12 below). [IAS 39.F.2.12]. Further discussion on the hedge documentation requirements for designated highly probable forecast transactions is given in section 6.3.3 below. 2.6.2 Hedged items held at fair value through profit or loss It does not immediately appear that it would be useful to designate a hedged item that is measured at fair value through profit or loss in a hedge relationship. However, because IFRS 9 could require certain variable rate assets to be measured at fair value through profit or loss (see Chapter 44 at section 5), designation as the hedged item in a cash flow hedge relationship could be desirable. Although the variable hedged item would be measured at fair value through profit or loss, an entity could still seek to hedge the variability of cash flows by entering into a hedging derivative. Because of the variable nature of the hedged item, such instruments might not be significantly exposed to changes in fair value caused by movements in the hedged risk whereas the hedging derivative will be. In this instance, application of cash flow hedge accounting facilitates deferral of fair value changes in the hedging derivative to the cash flow hedge reserve, which could better reflect the risk management strategy. An additional reason that a hedge designation might be desirable for hedged items measured at fair value through profit or loss could be in order to achieve costs of hedging treatment for the time value, forward or foreign currency basis element of the hedging instrument (see section 7.5 below). For any such designation care would need to be taken that the usual qualifying criteria for hedge accounting are met (see section 6 below). IFRS 9 provides confirmation that cash flow hedge accounting is not prohibited for all hedged items measured at fair value through profit or loss. It gives as an example of an eligible cash flow hedge where an entity uses a swap to change floating rate debt to fixed rate, even if the debt is measured at fair value. This is because there is a systematic way in which the cash flow hedge reserve can be reclassified, i.e., in the same way interest payments occur on the hedged instrument. A contrasting example is given of a forecast purchase of an equity instrument that, once acquired, will be accounted for at fair value through profit or loss. This is mentioned as an example of an item that cannot be the hedged item in a cash flow hedge, because any gain or loss on the hedging instrument that would be deferred could not be appropriately reclassified to profit or loss during a period in which it would achieve offset. [IFRS 9.B6.5.2]. As well as providing confirmation that cash flow hedge accounting is not precluded for hedged items measured at fair value through profit or loss, the guidance in IFRS 9.B6.5.2 also appears to introduce an additional requirement that there is a systematic way in which the cash flow hedge reserve can be reclassified for such a hedge. [IFRS 9.B6.5.2]. Therefore, it seems that cash flow hedge accounting is not only precluded for equity instruments but also for any non-financial hedged items measured at fair value through profit or loss. The same difficulty with respect to a lack of a systematic way in which the cash flow hedge reserve would be reclassified also exists in a cash flow hedge of a forecast transaction to purchase equity instruments or non-financial hedged items that will be measured at fair value through profit or loss. In addition, the reason why the instrument is measured at fair value through profit or loss will also be a factor in determining whether an instrument measured at fair value through profit or loss will be an eligible hedged item or not. Classification of an item at fair value through profit or loss could be because such an item is held for trading, managed on a fair value basis, designated as measured at fair value through profit or loss using the fair value option or because the contractual terms of the financial asset give rise on specified dates to cash flows that are not solely payments of principal and interest on the principal amount outstanding (see Chapter 44 at section 1). We discuss the eligibility of such instruments measured at fair value through profit or loss in the paragraphs below.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting 2.6.2.A Hedged items managed on a fair value basis If an instrument is part of a portfolio that is managed on a fair value basis, there is a question as to whether it can be an eligible hedged item or not.
