Guidance is provided on the accounting for the amount that has been accumulated in the cash flow hedge reserve on discontinuation of hedge accounting (see section 8.3.2 below). [IFRS 9.6.5.12].
IFRS 18 Presentation and Disclosure in Financial Statements, which supersedes IAS 1 for reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027 (with earlier application permitted) was issued by the IASB in April 2024. See Chapter 4 for further details on the new concepts and requirements of IFRS 18.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting 7.2.3 Recycling for a hedge of foreign currency monetary items It was stated in section 5.2.3 above that using a forward foreign currency contract to hedge a foreign currency payable or receivable could be treated either as a fair value hedge, or a cash flow hedge. In a fair value hedge, the gain or loss on remeasurement of both the forward contract and the hedged item are recognised immediately in profit or loss. Even if the forward foreign currency risk was the designated hedged risk, then ineffectiveness would arise from changes in the forward points, as the retranslation of the foreign currency payable or receivable is restricted to the IAS 21 spot revaluation (see section 7.1.1 above). [IFRS 9.B6.2.3]. However, in a cash flow hedge of the forward foreign currency risk, the cumulative gain or loss on remeasuring the forward contract is recognised in the cash flow hedge reserve (assuming no sources of ineffectiveness) and reclassified from the cash flow hedge reserve to profit or loss when the payable or receivable affects profit or loss (see section 7.2.2 above). Because the payable or receivable is remeasured continuously in respect of changes in foreign currency rates per IAS 21, there is a requirement for the gain or loss on the forward contract to be reclassified from the cash flow hedge reserve to profit or loss as the payable or receivable is remeasured, not when the payment occurs. There is variation in practice as to the method used to facilitate the reclassification over the period the payable or receivable affects profit or loss (see Illustration 7-18 in section 7.4.7 below). The forward element of a forward contract can be excluded from a designated fair value or cash flow hedge relationship (designation of the spot exchange risk only - see section 3.6.5 above). In that case any fair value changes attributable to the forward element are recognised in other comprehensive income if accounted for as costs of hedging (see sections 7.4.7 and 7.5.2 below). [IFRS 9.6.5.16]. 7.2.4 Cash flow hedges within subsidiaries on acquisition or disposal Where a reporting entity acquires a subsidiary that is applying cash flow hedge accounting, additional considerations arise. In applying the acquisition method of accounting in its consolidated financial statements, the reporting entity does not inherit the subsidiary’s existing cash flow hedge reserve, since this clearly represents cumulative pre-acquisition gains and losses (see Chapter 10 at section 5.4). This has implications for the assessment of hedge effectiveness and the measurement of ineffectiveness because, so far as the group is concerned, it has effectively started a new hedge relationship with a hedging instrument that is likely to have a non-zero fair value (see section 6.3.1 above and section 7.4.3 below). Where a subsidiary is acquired as part of a transaction under common control and the acquisition method of accounting is not applied the situation is different (see Chapter 11 at section 3.3.4). The standard does not address the situation when it is anticipated that a subsidiary will be disposed of. In November 2016, the IFRS IC discussed the issue of whether a group must discontinue hedge accounting in the consolidated financial statements where the group applies cash flow hedge accounting to forecast transactions that are anticipated in a subsidiary after the expected date of disposal of that subsidiary. The IFRS IC were of the tentative view that the assessment of a qualifying hedging relationship must be performed from the group’s perspective based on whether the transaction is highly probable and could affect the group’s profit or loss. Given that the forecast transactions are expected to occur only after the expected date of disposal of the subsidiary, these transactions are no longer expected to occur from the group’s perspective as soon as the subsidiary is classified as held for sale. According to the IFRS IC’s tentative view the forecast transactions would no longer be eligible hedged items and the group would discontinue hedge accounting from the date the subsidiary is classified as held for sale.20 The IFRS IC suggested that outreach would be helpful to understand if diverse accounting is currently applied in practice and is likely to be an issue to be considered in the Post Implementation Review of the IFRS 9 hedge accounting guidance. If the IFRS IC’s view is confirmed, existing accounting policies might need to be changed retrospectively, although this has still not happened at the time of writing.
IFRIC Update, November 2016, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting 7.3 Accounting for hedges of a net investment in a foreign operation Hedges of a net investment in a foreign operation (see section 5.3 above), including a hedge of a monetary item that is accounted for as part of the net investment (see section 5.3.2 above and Chapter 16 at section 6.3.1), shall be accounted for similarly to cash flow hedges: • The portion of the gain or loss on the hedging instrument that is determined to be an effective hedge must be recognised in other comprehensive income and (as clarified by IFRIC 16) included with the foreign exchange differences arising on translation of the results and financial position of the foreign operation. [IFRIC 16.3]. And • The ineffective portion shall be recognised in profit or loss. [IFRS 9.6.5.13]. Accordingly, as a result of accounting for hedges of a net investment there is no change to the usual IAS 21 requirement to retranslate the net investment at the closing spot rate (see Chapter 16 at section 6.1). [IAS 21.39]. Consequently, as for cash flow hedge accounting, accounting for a net investment hedge only has the potential to change the accounting for the hedging instrument and only to the extent of the effective portion. The cumulative gain or loss on the hedging instrument relating to the effective portion of the hedge that has been accumulated in the foreign currency translation reserve shall be reclassified from equity to profit or loss as a reclassification adjustment on disposal or, in certain circumstances, partial disposal of the foreign operation in accordance with IAS 21 (see Chapter 16 at section 6.6). [IFRS 9.6.5.14]. The meaning of ‘similarly to cash flow hedges’ is not immediately clear and has been the subject of some debate. It is readily understood that the portion of the gain or loss on the hedging derivative that is determined to be an effective hedge must be recognised in other comprehensive income (as it would for a cash flow hedge). However, the wording in the standard also seems to indicate that ineffectiveness must be measured in the same way as for cash flow hedges, i.e., no ineffectiveness is recognised in profit or loss if the gain or loss on the hedging instrument is less, in absolute terms, than the gain or loss on the hedged item (see section 7.2.1 above). This is despite the fact that there appears to be no good reason why ineffectiveness is not also recognised in profit or loss if the gain or loss on the hedging instrument is less, in absolute terms, than the gain or loss on the hedged item. This is different to the accounting for net investment hedges under US GAAP,21 for which it is clear that ineffectiveness must be recognised in profit or loss for under-hedges as well as over-hedges. In its March 2016 meeting, the IFRS IC discussed this issue. The IFRS IC concluded that the guidance in IFRS 9 is sufficiently clear and would require an entity to apply the lower of test for net investment hedges. One of the arguments put forward was that the application of the lower of test in determining the effective portion of the gains or losses arising from the hedging instrument when accounting for net investment hedges, avoids the recycling of exchange differences arising from the hedged item that have been recognised in other comprehensive income before the disposal of the foreign operation. Such an outcome would be consistent with the requirements of IAS 21.22 Whilst the above accounting might appear relatively straight forward at first consideration, a number of additional complexities arise in the accounting for net investment hedges. The complexity is largely driven by the requirement to apply guidance written for a cash flow hedge to a situation where no cash flow arises, and the accounting for net investments in foreign operations within complex groups. These are further considered in the following sections. An overview of the various hedge designation alternatives for net investment hedges is also provided in section 7.3.4 below. 7.3.1 Identifying the effective portion in a net investment hedge In accounting for a hedge of a net investment, IFRS 9 requires that the portion of the gain or loss on the hedging instrument that is determined to be an effective hedge must be recognised in other comprehensive income. [IFRS 9.6.5.13]. IFRIC 16 provides some helpful guidance in calculating the effective portion of the gain or loss on the hedging instrument. As set out in section 5.3.1 above, IFRIC 16 explains that the hedged risk in a net investment hedge is defined by reference to the functional currency of a parent of the foreign operation that is the subject of the hedge. The cumulative gain or loss on the hedging instrument that is determined to be effective is the
ASC 815-35-35-1 through 35-26 (formerly, Statement 133 Implementation Issue H8, Foreign Currency Hedges: Measuring the Amount of Ineffectiveness in a Net Investment Hedge). IFRIC Update, March 2016, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting change in value of the hedging instrument in respect of foreign exchange risk, this must also be computed by reference to the functional currency of this parent entity. [IFRIC 16.15]. Depending on where the hedging instrument is held, in the absence of hedge accounting the total change in its value might be recognised in profit or loss, in other comprehensive income, or both (see Illustration 7-8 below). The interpretation states that the calculation of the effective portion is not affected by where the change in value of the hedging instrument would be recognised. In applying hedge accounting, the total effective portion of the change must be included in other comprehensive income. [IFRIC 16.15]. Illustration 7-7: Identification of the effective portion in a net investment hedge (1) Consider the situation outlined in Illustration 5-3 above (see section 5.3.1 above). In summary. Company P is the ultimate parent entity of a group and presents its consolidated financial statements in its functional currency of the euro. It has two direct wholly owned subsidiaries, Company A whose functional currency is Japanese yen and Company B whose functional currency is sterling. B has a wholly owned subsidiary, Company C, whose functional currency is US dollars. P’s net investment in A is ¥400,000 million. P’s net investment in B is £500 million including the equivalent of £159 million representing B’s net investment in C of US$300 million. Also assume in this example that A has external borrowings of US$300 million and that A’s borrowings are designated in a hedge of the €/US$ spot risk associated with P’s net investment in C. In the absence of hedge accounting, the total US$/€ foreign exchange difference on A’s US$300 million external borrowing would be recognised in P’s consolidated financial statements as follows: [IFRIC 16.AG5] • US$/¥ spot foreign exchange rate change, translated to euro, in profit or loss And • ¥/€ spot foreign exchange rate change in other comprehensive income The fact that gain or loss on the hedging instrument is split between profit or loss and OCI does not affect the calculation of the effective portion in applying paragraph 6.5.13 of IFRS 9. The cumulative gain or loss on the hedging instrument that is determined to be effective is computed by reference to the functional currency of P, the hedging parent, which is euro. [IFRIC 16.15]. Hence, according to the guidance, all of the €/US$ foreign exchange difference on A’s US$300 million external borrowing would, after the application of hedge accounting, be recognised in other comprehensive income and be included in the foreign currency translation reserve relating to C. The guidance says this is because the change in value of both the hedging instrument and the hedged item are computed by reference to the functional currency of P (euro) against the functional currency of C (US dollars).
**[IFRIC 16.AG4, IFRIC 16.AG7].**
IFRIC 16 doesn’t include a worked example. We have simplified the illustration by only revaluing the US$ liability at year end and assumed the following spot exchange rates:

Illustration 7-8: Identification of the effective portion in a net investment hedge (2) Using the same fact pattern as in Illustration 7-7 above, except, P designated its own US$ denominated borrowings in a hedge of the spot foreign exchange exposure (£/US$) between B and C, only the £/US$ part of the change in the value of the borrowings (the effective portion of the hedging instrument) would be included in P’s foreign currency translation reserve relating to C. The remainder of the change (equivalent to the £/€ change on £159 million) would be included in P’s consolidated profit or loss. [IFRIC 16.AG12]. The calculation of the effective portion is not affected by whether the hedging instrument is a derivative or a non-derivative instrument or by the method of consolidation. [IFRIC 16.15]. Hence whether the direct or step-by-step method of consolidation is applied, the effective portion will be calculated in the same way (see Chapter 16 at sections 6.1.4 and 6.6). It should be noted however that the method of consolidation can result in a potential difference in the accounting outcome on disposal of a foreign operation (see section 8.3.8 below). 7.3.2 Non-derivative liabilities hedging a net investment A foreign currency denominated non-derivative financial liability, such as a borrowing, can be used as the hedging instrument in a hedge of a net investment in a foreign operation (see section 3.3.1 above). [IFRIC 16.14]. This can be seen as purely an ‘accounting’ hedge, i.e., the retranslation gain or loss on the borrowing (an accounting entry representing a part of its change in fair value that is accounted for on a continuous basis) can offset the retranslation gain or loss on the net investment (another accounting entry). In fact, if the liability is: • Denominated in the same currency as the functional currency of the hedged net investment • Held by an entity with the same functional currency as the parent by which the hedged risk is defined • Has an amortised cost that is lower than the net investment in the foreign operation And • Is designated appropriately Then the hedge is likely to be perfectly effective in terms of the offsetting retranslation gains and losses on the liability and the hedged proportion of the net investment. If a borrowing or similar liability is denominated in a different currency to the functional currency of the net investment, it might still be possible to designate it as the hedging instrument. However, it will need to be demonstrated that the two currencies are sufficiently correlated so that the hedging instrument is expected to result in offsetting gains and losses over the period that the hedge is designated, i.e., an economic relationship exists (see section 6.4.1 above). This might be the case if the two currencies are formally pegged or otherwise linked to one another or if the relevant exchange rates move in tandem because of, say, similarities in the underlying economies. Even if such a hedge meets the hedge effectiveness requirements (see section 6.4 above), it is likely to result in some ineffectiveness. Under US GAAP it is suggested that the retranslation gains and losses on the actual instrument must be compared to those on a hypothetical non-derivative (e.g., a borrowing in the correct currency) with any difference recognised in profit or loss.23 This approach will normally be acceptable under IFRS 9, if applied in conjunction with the accounting requirements for net investment hedges (see section 7.3 above). For hedges of foreign currency risk, the foreign currency risk component of a non-derivative financial instrument is determined in accordance with IAS 21. [IFRS 9.B6.2.3]. Therefore, when designating a foreign currency denominated non-derivative financial liability as the hedging instrument in a hedge of a net investment, it is only possible to designate the spot foreign exchange risk, and not a forward foreign exchange risk (see section 7.3.4 below). [IFRIC 16.AG2].
ASC 815-35-35-1 to 35-26 (formerly, Statement 133 Implementation Issue H8, Foreign Currency Hedges: Measuring the Amount of Ineffectiveness in a Net Investment Hedge).
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting 7.3.3 Derivatives hedging a net investment It is clear that a derivative instrument may be designated as the hedging instrument in a hedge of a net investment. [IFRIC 16.14]. What is harder to determine is how various types of derivatives might be designated in a hedge of a net investment in order that the hedge relationship meets the usual hedge accounting criteria, and how the relationship must be accounted for. For example: • Is it possible to designate the forward foreign currency risk as the hedged risk in a net investment hedge? • How should the hedged risk in the net investment be represented for ineffectiveness measurement purposes when the hedging instrument is a derivative? • What is the accounting treatment for any excluded portions of a derivative hedging instrument in a net investment hedge? These questions will be considered in the remainder of this section. In the application guidance to IFRIC 16, it is noted that if the hedging instrument is a forward contract, then it is possible to designate a forward foreign currency risk as the hedged risk in a hedge of a net investment (see section 3.6.5 above). [IFRIC 16.AG2]. Forward foreign currency risk must therefore be deemed to exist within the net investment. If the forward foreign currency risk is designated for a net investment hedge, then the entire change in fair value of the hedging instrument (including changes in the forward element) must be included in the calculation of the portion of the gain or loss on the hedging instrument that is determined to be an effective hedge. [IFRS 9.6.5.13]. This could be achieved by using a hypothetical derivative that is also a forward foreign contract (see sections 7.3.4 and 7.4.4 below). Like forward contracts, cross-currency interest rate swaps are sometimes used as hedging instruments in net investment hedges. It is often said that fixed-for-fixed cross-currency swaps are essentially a series of forward contracts and so arguably the treatment for forwards is equally applicable to cross-currency swaps. Therefore, it will also be possible for a fixed-for fixed cross-currency swap to be a good representation of the forward foreign currency risk in a net investment hedge (see sections 7.3.3.B and 7.3.4 below). There is no reason why an option contract, that is not a written option, cannot be designated as the hedging instrument in a hedge of a net investment (see section 3.2.2 above). In fact, such a hedge is included as an example in the IFRS 9 application guidance. [IFRS 9.B6.5.29(b)]. However, it will most likely be helpful to designate only the change in intrinsic value of the option as the hedging instrument, as the time value does not exist in the hedged item (see section 7.5.1 below). It is also possible to designate only the spot foreign currency risk associated with a derivative hedging instrument in a net investment hedge, thereby excluding other elements (e.g., the forward element) from the hedge relationship. [IFRS 9.6.2.4(b)]. In that case the hypothetical derivative would be a spot foreign currency contract. Even when only spot risk is hedged, there is a requirement to consider the time value of money when measuring ineffectiveness (see sections 7.4.2 and 7.4.7 below). However, in the case of a hedge of a net investment, for which there are no cash flows or maturity date with respect to the hedged item, the concept of discounting the spot element back from the date the hedged item is expected to occur has no relevance. A net investment hedge is generally viewed as a hedge of an ‘accounting’ outcome, i.e., to offset the IAS 21 retranslation gain or loss on the net investment. In that sense the hedged item has already occurred and so the effect of the time value of money is zero (i.e., a discount factor of 1 is appropriate). Hence in the hedge of spot foreign currency risk within a net investment, and only in such a hedge, no discounting of the spot revaluation is required for the purposes of measuring ineffectiveness (see section 7.3.4 below). Where such portions are excluded, they could be treated as costs of hedging (see section 7.5 below) or, alternatively, the excluded forward element of forwards and/or foreign currency basis spread could remain at fair value through profit or loss. [IFRS 9.6.5.16]. There is no choice of treatment for the excluded time value of options, as they must be treated as a cost of hedging. [IFRS 9.6.5.15]. If using a derivative in the hedge of a net investment of a foreign operation, as foreign currency basis spread cannot be assumed to exist in a non-derivative hedged item, ineffectiveness will occur unless the cost of hedging treatment is applied to the foreign currency basis spread (see section 7.4.8 below). This treatment for hedges of a net investment is discussed in more detail in section 7.5.2.A below. Given the variety of hedging instruments and designation choices available in the hedge of a net investment and also considering the limited specific guidance available within IFRS 9 on this topic, we provide more discussion in the rest of this section, focusing on the different hedging instruments.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting Additional application guidance is provided in section 7.3.4 below, illustrating the different designation approaches in the hedge of a net investment and the associated accounting consequences. 7.3.3.A Forward currency contracts hedging a net investment It is very common for forward currency contracts to be used as the hedging instrument in a hedge of a net investment and, as noted above, it is possible to designate a forward foreign currency risk for such a hedge (see section 7.3.3 above). [IFRIC 16.AG2]. In that case, the hedged foreign currency risk in the net investment hedge would ordinarily be represented by a hypothetical forward foreign currency contract. If on the other hand the spot risk is designated (i.e., the hedged foreign currency risk in the net investment hedge is represented by a hypothetical spot foreign currency exposure), the costs of hedging guidance could be applied to the excluded portion rather than fair value changes in the excluded portion being recognised immediately in profit or loss (see section 7.5 below). Under either designation, the minimum amount of net investment required to match the hedging instrument would be an amount equal to the foreign currency leg of the forward currency contract (see section 7.3.4 below). It is clear under IFRS 9 that foreign currency basis spread cannot be assumed to exist in a non-derivative hedged item (including a foreign currency net investment), hence this will be an additional source of ineffectiveness, unless foreign currency basis spread is excluded from the hedging instrument (see sections 7.4.8 and 7.5.3 below). [IFRS 9.B6.5.5]. 7.3.3.B Cross-currency interest rate swaps hedging a net investment Like forward contracts, cross-currency interest rate swap instruments are commonly used as hedging instruments in net investment hedges. However, the general suitability of a cross-currency swap as the hedging instrument in the hedge accounting of a net investment and what the appropriate hypothetical derivative would be for measuring ineffectiveness, depends very much on the type of cross-currency interest rate swap used. At a conceptual level, it is easy to see that the changes in value of a cross-currency swap with two floating- rate legs are likely to offset the spot retranslation gains and losses of a net investment, provided the floating rate resets sufficiently frequently. Hence such a designation will most probably meet the requirement to demonstrate that an economic relationship exists, although some residual ineffectiveness is likely to occur from fair value changes in the most recently reset floating rate legs of the cross-currency swap and also from foreign currency basis spread (if not excluded from the hedge relationship (see sections 7.3.4, 7.4.8 and 7.5.2.A below)). With the transition from term IBOR rates to the use of daily compounding overnight risk-free rates as part of IBOR reform, ineffectiveness caused by floating legs will be significantly reduced (see the Supplement: IBOR Reform). Although the forward element is not explicitly defined by the standard, it is explained that the difference between the forward rate and the spot rate (i.e., ‘the forward element’) represents the interest differential between the two currencies at inception (see section 3.6.5 above). [IFRS 9.BC6.424]. Accordingly, as the interest differential in a cross-currency swap with two floating-rate legs is not set at inception, it appears that it is not possible to designate the forward foreign currency risk for such a hedging instrument (see section 7.3.4 below). It is also reasonably easy to see that a swap with one floating-rate leg and one fixed-rate leg could result in levels of ineffectiveness that would contradict the existence of an economic relationship between the hedged item and hedging instrument, which would preclude hedge accounting (see section 6.4.1 above). This is because the fair value of a swap with one floating-rate leg and one fixed-rate leg will change due to factors unrelated to changes in forward exchange rates. As noted previously (see section 7.3.3 above), a fixed-for-fixed cross-currency swap is often viewed as a combination of a series of forward contracts, (i.e., each interest and principal fixed foreign currency cash flow and associated fixed functional currency cash flow is a forward contract, albeit based on a blended forward rate across the life of the instrument). Accordingly, it would make sense that designation of either the forward foreign currency risk or the spot foreign currency risk is permitted. This is well established in practice and is relatively uncontroversial. However, variation in practice does arise in how the spot and forward elements are identified when using a fixed-for-fixed cross-currency swap in the hedge of a net investment. This variation in practice is partly driven by the fact that IFRS 9 requires that net investment hedges shall be accounted for similarly to cash flow hedges. [IFRS 9.6.5.13]. This guidance, which requires the existence of cash flows within hedged items, therefore requires some interpretation when it is applied to hedged items for which no cash flows exist.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting One alternative is to designate the fixed-for-fixed cross-currency swap, such that the amount of the hedged item would be equal to the sum of the undiscounted foreign currency interest and principal payments, (i.e., an amount higher than just the notional amount of the swap). This approach is consistent with a cash flow hedge view, since it best matches the cash flows on the swap, but arguably overstates the foreign exchange risk as it is based on undiscounted foreign currency exposures. Another approach would be to designate the fixed-for-fixed cross-currency swap, such that the amount of the hedged item would equal just the undiscounted foreign currency principal payment (i.e., the notional amount of the swap). This approach is based on the view that the interest payments on the cross-currency swap economically represent the forward interest element within the swap, and so will not form part of the spot element. [IFRS 9.BC6.416]. How we see it We believe it is acceptable to designate amounts equal to either the sum of a fixed-for-fixed cross- currency swap’s undiscounted foreign currency interest and principal payments, or just its notional principal, as the hedged item in a net investment hedge. Both views are explained further in Illustration 7-9 below. Illustration 7-9: Hedge of a foreign currency net investment using a cross-currency swap Entity P, functional currency euro, has a £120m foreign currency net investment in Subsidiary S. S’s functional currency is GBP. In order to hedge a proportion of the foreign currency EUR/GBP translation risk associated with P’s net investment in S, P has transacted a 2 year fixed-for-fixed cross-currency swap as follows: a foreign currency leg with a notional amount of £100 million, paying 3% annually, and a functional currency leg with €110m notional, receiving 2.5% annually. P chooses to designate the forward foreign currency risk in £106 million of the net investment (i.e., a proportion of the net investment is hedged by each foreign currency cash flow arising from the cross-currency swap, in this case the £100 million plus the two payments of interest of £3 million). The foreign currency risk for such a designated hedged item could be represented by a fixed-for-fixed cross-currency swap (this would be for both the hypothetical derivative used to measure the effective portion and the aligned hedging derivative if the costs of hedging guidance is applied to the cross-currency basis spread) (see sections 7.3.4, 7.4.4. and 7.5.2 below). If cost of hedging is not applied, then the changes in the fair value of the cross- currency swap due to cross-currency basis spreads would be recognised in profit or loss. [IFRS 9.B6.5.5]. If P were to choose to designate the spot foreign currency risk of the £106 million of net investment, only the undiscounted spot revaluation of the aggregate of the swap foreign currency notional and interest payments would form part of the hedge relationship (see section 3.6.5 above). As no sources of ineffectiveness exist in this fact pattern, other changes in the fair value of the cross-currency swap would form part of the cost of hedging for the forward element and cross-currency basis spreads, if cost of hedging accounting is applied. If cost of hedging is not applied, then the other changes in the fair value of the cross-currency swap would be recognised in profit or loss. Alternatively, P could choose to designate the fixed-for-fixed cross-currency swap with a foreign currency notional amount of £100 million as a hedge of the forward foreign currency risk in a net investment of only £100 million. In that case, (similar to the designation of £106 million of net investment above) the foreign currency risk for such a designated hedged item could be represented by a fixed-for-fixed cross-currency swap (this would be for both the hypothetical derivative used to measure the effective portion and the aligned hedging derivative if the costs of hedging guidance is applied to the cross-currency basis spreads). If cost of hedging is not applied, then the changes in the fair value of the cross-currency swap due to cross-currency basis spreads would be recognised in profit or loss. If P were to choose to designate the spot foreign currency risk only of the £100 million net investment, only the undiscounted spot revaluation of the swap foreign currency notional would form part of the hedge relationship. As no sources of ineffectiveness exist in this fact pattern, other changes in the cross-currency swap fair value will form part of the cost of hedging for the forward element and cross-currency basis spreads. If the cost of hedging guidance is not applied, then the other changes in fair value of the cross-currency swap would be recognised in profit or loss. See section 7.3.4 below for a further illustration of the different designation possibilities. It can be seen from the above Illustrations that if there are no sources of ineffectiveness, then volatility in profit or loss from fair value changes in the hedging cross-currency swap can be eliminated under all designation alternatives if the cost of hedging guidance is also applied. However, given that volatility in profit or loss from changes in fair value due to cross-currency basis spreads can only be eliminated if the cost of hedging guidance is applied, it might be operationally simpler to hedge only the spot foreign currency risk, and apply the cost of hedging guidance to both the forward element and the cross-currency
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting basis spreads (see section 7.5.3.A below). This will result in both being amortised to profit or loss on a systematic and rational basis. However, under a forward foreign currency risk designation, the forward element would remain in foreign currency reserve, until the net investment was disposed of (see section 8.3.8 below). 7.3.3.C Purchased options hedging a net investment It is possible to designate the intrinsic value of a purchased option as the hedging instrument (i.e., its time value is excluded from the hedge relationship) (see section 3.6.4 above). Such a designation would require the costs of hedging treatment to be applied. The costs of hedging accounting would require changes in fair value of the excluded time value to be initially recorded in other comprehensive income to the extent it relates to the hedged item (see section 7.5.1 below). As discussed in section 3.6.4 above, designating the entire purchased option as the hedging instrument is likely to result in high levels of ineffectiveness and could challenge the existence of an economic relationship (see section 6.4.1 above). This is because no offset will arise for changes in the time value of the purchased option, as the time value is not a component of the hedged net investment. [IFRS 9.B6.3.12]. 7.3.4 Overview of alternatives for designation of a net investment hedge As can be seen from the sections above there are a variety of different ways in which a net investment (NI) hedge can be designated. In order to compare and contrast the different outcomes for net investment hedge accounting, we set out below a high-level summary of some of the more common strategies for a net investment hedge. In the examples below within Figures 7-1, 7-2 and 7-3, for simplicity, we have assumed that there are no sources of ineffectiveness, including from foreign currency basis spreads. However, given that an assumption of no ineffectiveness from foreign currency basis spreads is an unlikely scenario, an indication of the effect of foreign currency basis spread is provided in the footnotes below the tables (also see section 7.4.8 below).
