Hedging instrument Profit or loss profile after hedge accounting (14) (6) N/A Forward element amortised to P&L over the life of the FX forward Floating legs recognised in P&L, including some fair value volatility from coupon fixings over time Cross-currency basis spread amortised to P&L over the life of the swap (13) Fixed legs interest recognised in P&L over the life of the hedge OCI profile after hedge accounting N/A Some volatility over the life of the hedge from forward element, but net nil on maturity of foreign currency forward contract Some volatility over the life of the hedge from foreign currency basis spreads, but net nil on maturity of hedging swap Some volatility over the life of the hedge from discounting of fixed legs, but net nil on maturity of hedging swap (1) In the hedge of spot foreign currency risk within a NI, and only in such a hedge, no discounting of the spot revaluation is required for the purposes of measuring ineffectiveness as the hedged item does not have any cash flows or maturity date to discount back from (see section 7.3.3 above). (2) Although in most cases the hypothetical derivative will take the same form as the hedging instrument (i.e., debt, foreign currency forward, foreign currency swap etc.) it is clear that the terms of the hypothetical derivative are based on the hedged item (NI in this case). Hence if there is a mismatch between the designated amount of NI and the hedging instrument, then ineffectiveness could arise as the hypothetical and actual hedging instrument will not be the same (see section 7.4.4 below). (3) As per 7.3.3.B. (4) The amounts in OCI will not be the same under designation a) and b). This is because the amount of designated NI is different under each designation, and so the amount of offset from the undiscounted spot revaluation of the designated NI will also be different. (5) Where the hedging instrument is a non-derivative, the foreign currency risk component of a non-derivative financial instrument is determined in accordance with IAS 21 (see section 7.3.2 above). (6) The above scenarios all assume no sources of ineffectiveness. Where sources of ineffectiveness do arise (e.g., insufficient NI is designated) some additional P&L volatility might arise, subject to the ‘lower of assessment’. (7) Although the above assumes that foreign currency basis does not exist, in reality profit or loss volatility would also arise from changes in fair value in the hedging instrument due to foreign currency basis spreads (unless the costs of hedging treatment is applied) (see sections 7.4.8 and 7.5 below). (8) The amounts in P&L will not be the same under designation a) and b). This is because under designation a), in addition to the spot revaluation of the foreign currency principal being recognised in OCI, the undiscounted spot revaluation of the foreign currency coupons will also be recognised there rather than P&L. However, this additional spot revaluation will be offset in OCI by the additional amount of NI designated under designation a). (9) Designation of the forward element is not permitted when hedging with a non-derivative (see section 7.3.2 above). [IFRS 9.B6.2.3, IFRIC 16.AG2]. (10) As the forward element has been designated, the risk management objective must be to hedge the forward foreign currency risk over the life of the hedging instrument - in that case it is appropriate to infer a forward date for the purposes of hedge accounting. [IFRIC 16.AG2]. (11) As the forward element in a floating-floating cross-currency swap has not been locked in, this is not a hedge of a forward rate, hence there is no forward element to designate (see section 7.3.3.B above). [IFRS 9.6.2.4(b), IFRS 9.BC6.426]. (12) There are no eligible cost of hedging within a non-derivative hedging instrument, so designation is not permitted (see section 7.5 below). (13) As the forward element in a floating-floating cross-currency swap has not been locked in, it is not possible to apply the costs of hedging guidance to a forward element - however the cost of hedging guidance is relevant for foreign currency basis (see sections 7.4.8 and 7.5.2 below).
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting (14) Although the above assumes that foreign currency basis does not exist, it is possible to apply the costs of hedging guidance to foreign currency basis under this designation, in the same way as for the forward element. (15) Although when hedging NI it will be common for the aligned instrument to have the same terms as the hedging instrument, if there is insufficient NI and/or the hedging derivative did not have a zero fair value on designation, some differences will arise that could result in additional P&L volatility (see section 7.5.1.A below). 7.3.5 Combinations of derivative and non-derivative instruments hedging a net investment It is not uncommon for entities to hedge their net investments using synthetic foreign currency debt instruments. For example, consider a parent with the euro as its functional currency that has a net investment in a Japanese subsidiary with yen as its functional currency. The parent might borrow in US dollars and enter into a pay-Japanese yen, receive-US dollar cross-currency interest rate swap. In this way the two instruments might be considered a synthetic Japanese yen borrowing, although they are required to be accounted for separately (see Chapter 42 at section 7). As noted in section 3.5 above, a combination of derivatives and non-derivatives may be viewed in combination and jointly designated as a hedging instrument. [IFRS 9.6.2.5]. However, all the hedging instruments must be clearly identified in the hedge documentation (see section 6.3 above). [IFRS 9.6.4.1(b)]. The European parent in the above fact pattern might wish to designate the spot rate only when hedging a net investment with a combination of derivatives and non-derivatives. Designation of the combination as hedging a forward rate is more problematic as the foreign currency risk component of a non-derivative financial instrument is restricted to the spot rate, in accordance with IAS 21 (see sections 3.3.1 and 7.3.4 above). [IFRS 9.B6.2.3]. Another alternative could be to notionally decompose the cross-currency swap by introducing an interest bearing functional currency denominated leg and designating one part as a hedge of the borrowing and the other as a hedge of the net investment (see section 3.6.2 above). 7.3.6 Individual or separate financial statements The discussion so far has concentrated on the accounting for a hedge of the foreign currency risk in a foreign operation in the parent’s consolidated financial statements. However, all references to consolidated financial statements in the guidance on accounting for net investment hedges apply equally to standalone financial statements in which the net assets of a foreign operation are included. This will include financial statements in which investments in a foreign operation that is a joint venture, an associate or a branch are accounted for using the equity method. [IFRIC 16.2, IFRIC 16.7]. In a parent’s individual or separate financial statements there is an accounting policy choice as to how the investment in the foreign operation is accounted for: • As an asset measured at cost • As a financial asset in accordance with IFRS 9 Or • Using the equity method as described in IAS 28 Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures (see
# Chapter 12 at section 11) [IAS 27.10]
So although not explicitly stated in IFRIC 16, it would also be possible to apply the guidance on net investment hedges in standalone financial statements in which investments in subsidiaries are accounted for using the equity method. [IFRS 9.6.5.2(c), IAS 21.44, IAS 27.10(c)]. Where net investment hedges have been designated in a parent’s consolidated financial statements there is also the question of whether hedge accounting can be applied in that parent’s individual or separate financial statements if the equity method is not applied. This question is only likely to arise where an eligible hedging instrument exists within the parent entity (e.g., the parent entity has foreign currency external borrowings which are designated as the hedging instrument in a net investment hedge in the consolidated financial statements).
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting In the reporting entity’s individual or separate financial statements, if the accounting policy choice is to measure the investment either at cost or in accordance with IFRS 9, it is not accounted for as a net investment in a foreign operation and hence net investment hedge accounting is precluded. [IFRS 9.6.5.2(c), IAS 21.8]. This means a borrowing designated as the hedging instrument in a net investment hedge in the consolidated financial statements, could not be similarly designated in a net investment hedge for the purposes of the separate financial statements. However, if hedge accounting is desirable, it could be possible to designate the borrowing as the hedging instrument in another type of hedge relationship. How we see it It would in our view be possible to designate the foreign currency risk in the carrying value of the foreign currency denominated investment in the parent entity’s separate financial statements. Typically, this would be a fair value hedge of the foreign currency risk arising from the investment (see section 5.1 above). This will be an independent hedge relationship, separate from the net investment hedge in the consolidated financial statements. Therefore, all of the other hedge accounting criteria (including the documentation requirements) will need to be met for this hedge too. Of course, the effects of this hedge accounting will need to be reversed when preparing the group’s consolidated financial statements (otherwise those financial statements will reflect as an asset or liability certain changes in the fair value of a parent’s investment in its subsidiary which would be contrary to the general principles of IFRS 10 see
# Chapter 8).
7.4 Measuring ineffectiveness 7.4.1 General requirements Hedge ineffectiveness is the extent to which the changes in the fair value or cash flows of the hedging instrument are greater or less than those on the hedged item. [IFRS 9.B6.4.1]. All hedge ineffectiveness is recognised in the profit or loss for fair value hedges (except where the hedged item is an equity instrument measured at fair value through OCI) (see section 7.1.1 above). For cash flow and net investment hedges ineffectiveness is recognised in the profit or loss to the extent that the cumulative change in the fair value or cash flows of the hedging instrument are greater than those on the hedged item (see sections 7.2.1 and 7.3 above). Accordingly, the measurement of ineffectiveness is an important aspect of IFRS 9. [IFRS 9.6.5.8, IFRS 9.6.5.11, IFRS 9.6.5.13]. Although, for many hedge relationships, it will be acceptable to undertake a qualitative assessment as to whether the hedge effectiveness requirements are met (see section 6.4 above) there is still a requirement to measure and record ineffectiveness appropriately within the financial statements. Other differences arise between assessing effectiveness for hedge qualification purposes and measuring ineffectiveness for the purposes of accounting for effective hedges. For example, in the hedge of foreign currency risk in a net investment the accounting for the net investment is restricted to the IAS 21 retranslation at the closing spot rate, but this is not the case in assessing effectiveness (see section 7.3 above). In determining the ineffectiveness of a hedge relationship, it is important to closely follow the hedge designation. In particular it is necessary to determine the hedged risk, and whether any portions or proportions have been excluded from the hedged item (see sections 2.2 and 2.3 above) and/or the hedging instrument (see section 3.6 above). Hedge ineffectiveness only relates to those elements not excluded from the hedge relationship, and for the hedged item only, with respect to changes in the hedged risk, not the full price risk (see Illustration 7-4 in section 7.2.1 above). [IFRS 9.B6.3.11, IFRS 9.B6.4.1]. The initial designation and documentation of a hedge relationship is therefore very important and can in some cases significantly reduce the expected ineffectiveness levels from hedge relationships. However, it should be noted that not all sources of ineffectiveness can be eliminated via clever hedge designation. All sources of ineffectiveness must be documented as part of the hedge documentation. [IFRS 9.6.4.1(b)]. The calculation of ineffectiveness compares the monetary amount of the change in fair value of the hedging instrument with the monetary amount of the change in fair value or cash flows of the hedged item or transactions attributable to the hedged risk, over the assessment period.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting This is illustrated by the following simplified example: Illustration 7-10: Calculation of ineffectiveness An entity issues fixed rate debt at par of £100, with annual fixed interest of 3%. On the same day, the entity designates an existing interest rate swap in a fair value hedge relationship with the issued debt, for changes in SONIA. The existing swap has a notional of £100, a receive leg of 2.5% annually and a floating leg paying a compounded SONIA rate every 6 months. At the first quarterly reporting period after designation, (i.e., prior to settlement of any coupons on the debt or interest rate swap) the effectiveness would be calculated as follows:
Hedged item (issued debt) Hedging instrument (existing swap) Fair value on initial designation £100 CR £4.5 CR Fair value on first reporting date £107 CR £2.4 DR Change in fair value of the hedging instrument over the assessment period
£6.9 gain Change in fair value of the hedged item attributable to the hedged risk over the assessment period £7 loss
Hedge ineffectiveness is calculated by comparing the £6.9 gain on the hedging instrument with £7 loss on the hedged item over the assessment period. Whilst it might be relatively obvious how to calculate the change in fair value or cash flows of a hedged item with fixed flows for changes in the hedged risk since designation (typically a fair value hedge) (see section 5.1 above), it is less obvious how this might be achieved where the hedged flows are not fixed, in particular, as hedged items with variable flows do not tend to attract fair value risk. This is discussed in more detail in sections 7.4.4 and 7.4.6 below. 7.4.2 The time value of money Entities must consider the time value of money when measuring hedge ineffectiveness. This means that an entity must determine the value of the hedged item on a present value basis (thereby including the effect of the time value of money). [IFRS 9.B6.5.4]. The inclusion of the words “the changes in the fair value or cash flows” in the definition of ineffectiveness is unhelpful (see section 7.4.1 above), but the need for consideration of the time value of money is clear based on the noted application guidance of IFRS 9. In valuation practice, the effect of the time value of money is also included when measuring the fair value of financial instruments. Consequently, it is logical to apply the same principle to the hedged item as well. It is possible to designate a spot element of a hedged item if it can be determined to be an eligible risk component (see section 2.2 above). Whilst a spot designation is relatively commonplace for both foreign currency and commodity risk, the need to consider the time value of money for effectiveness purposes for spot designations has been a matter of some debate in the past. The IAS 39 Implementation Guidance contains an example in which an entity designates changes in the spot element only. The example indicates that the time value of money is still relevant for the measurement of ineffectiveness even when the spot element is designated in a hedge relationship. Although the example is based on the IAS 39 Implementation Guidance it is still relevant under IFRS 9 (see section 7.4.7 below). [IAS 39.F.5.6]. How we see it IFRS 9 requires that the time value of money is considered when measuring ineffectiveness. Accordingly, when measuring ineffectiveness in the hedge of changes in spot risk this too must be undertaken on a present value basis, which means the spot rates used will need to be discounted from the date of the hedged cash flow. There are a couple of exceptions to the requirement to use discounted spot when measuring ineffectiveness, such that the normal IAS 21 revaluation requirements are applied; in the hedge of foreign currency risk by a non-derivative financial instrument, and in the hedge of a net investment in a foreign operation, whereby the net investment and (if applicable) the non-derivative financial instrument are retranslated at the closing rate (an undiscounted spot rate) (see sections 3.3.1 and 7.3 above).
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting Illustration 7-11 below simply demonstrates how ineffectiveness can arise when there are differences in the timing of hedged and hedging cash flows, even when only hedging changes in spot risk. Illustration 7-11: Impact of time value of money when measuring ineffectiveness A manufacturing company in India, having the Indian Rupee as its functional currency, is expecting forecast sales in USD. The company assesses sales of USD1 million per month for the next twelve months to be highly probable and wishes to hedge the related foreign currency exposure. The company also holds a borrowing of USD20 million with a bullet repayment in fourteen months’ time. Instead of entering into foreign currency forward contracts, the company designates the US dollar borrowing as a hedging instrument in hedges of the spot risk of the monthly highly probable US dollar sales. When measuring hedge ineffectiveness, the revaluation of the forecast sales for foreign currency spot risk would have to be made on a discounted basis (i.e., a present value calculation reflecting the time between the reporting date and the individual future cash flow dates), whereas the revaluation of the hedging instrument would not (as this follows the requirements of IAS 21) (see section 3.3.1 above). [IFRS 9.B6.2.3]. Similarly, if instead of using a US dollar borrowing as the hedging instrument, the entity transacted a forward foreign currency contract to sell USD12 million for Indian Rupee in 12 months’ time, ineffectiveness will arise due to the differences in the timing of hedged and hedging cash flows. For the hedged cash flows, the revaluation for foreign currency spot risk would have to be a present value calculation reflecting the time between the reporting date and the individual future monthly cash flow dates. In the same way, to measure hedge effectiveness, the present value of the spot foreign currency revaluation of the hedging instrument would reflect the time value to the ultimate maturity of the forward contract, which is not the same as the timing of the hedged individual monthly forecast cash flows. The treatment of the excluded forward element of the forward contract is discussed further in section 7.5.2 below. The requirement to calculate ineffectiveness on a present value basis intuitively makes sense in cases where the cash flows of the hedged item and hedging instrument are not aligned. It would seem inappropriate to have no ineffectiveness under a spot designation, for example in the situation described in section 3.3.1 above, where a 7-year financial liability denominated in a foreign currency is used as the hedging instrument for a 12 month forecast sale in that foreign currency. See Illustrations 7-17 and 7-18 in section 7.4.7 below for detailed examples of the accounting for hedge relationships where the foreign currency spot risk is designated. 7.4.3 Hedging using instruments with a non-zero fair value There is no requirement for hedge accounting to be designated on initial recognition of either the hedged item or the hedging instrument. [IFRS 9.B6.5.28]. However, there is often a hidden danger when designating a derivative as a hedging instrument subsequent to its inception. For non-option derivatives, such as forwards or interest rate swaps, any fair value is likely to create ‘noise’ in measuring hedge ineffectiveness that might not be fully offset by changes in the hedged item, especially in the case of a cash flow hedge. This is because the derivative contains a ‘financing’ element (the initial fair value), gains and losses on which will not be replicated in the hedged item and therefore the hedge contains an inherent source of ineffectiveness. For example, if applying the hypothetical derivative method for measurement of ineffectiveness (see section 7.4.4 below) this financing element will be evident as the hypothetical derivative will be based on the prevailing market rates on designation, which will result in cash flows that differ from those of the actual now ‘off market’ derivative. When hedging with an in the money option contract (i.e., it has positive intrinsic value), a similar issue arises. If applying the hypothetical derivatives method for measurement of ineffectiveness, any existing intrinsic value of the hedging option will not be replicated in the hypothetical derivative as it represents changes in the hedged risk in the period prior to the hedge designation and so is not relevant for the hedge relationship (see section 7.4.4 below). Consequently, there is likely to be more ineffectiveness recognised and, in extremis, a quantitative assessment could be needed to demonstrate the existence of an economic relationship (see section 6.4.1 above). It depends on the circumstances whether hedge ineffectiveness arising from the financing element (or intrinsic value) on designation could have a magnitude that a qualitative assessment would not adequately capture. [IFRS 9.B6.4.15]. Only by coincidence will a derivative that was entered into previously still have a fair value that is zero, or close to zero, on the date of designation which would minimise this problem. In the case of a fair value hedge, it might be possible to designate a risk component that matches a non-option hedging instrument even though it has a non-zero fair value (see section 2.4.1 above). This situation can not only arise when a derivative is designated or redesignated in a hedging relationship subsequent to its initial recognition, but also in a business combination (see section 6.3.1 above). This same issue does not arise for hedged items that are designated after initial recognition, however difficulties can occur identifying eligible risk components (see section 2.4.1 above).
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting 7.4.4 Hypothetical derivatives A method commonly used in practice to calculate ineffectiveness of cash flow hedges (and net investment hedges) is the use of a so-called ‘hypothetical derivative’. The method involves establishing a notional derivative that has terms that match the critical terms of the hedged exposure (normally an interest rate swap or forward contract with no unusual terms) and a zero fair value at inception of the hedging relationship. The fair value of the hypothetical derivative is then used as a proxy to measure the change in the value of the hedged item against which changes in value of the actual hedging instrument are compared to calculate ineffectiveness. The use of a hypothetical derivative is one possible way of determining the change in the value of the hedged item when measuring ineffectiveness. [IFRS 9.B6.5.5]. IFRS 9 is clear that a hypothetical derivative has to be a replication of the hedged item and not the ‘perfect hedge’. Also, the standard makes clear that any different method for determining the change in the value of the hedged item would have to give the same outcome. Consequently, an entity cannot include features in the hypothetical derivative that only exist in the hedging instrument, but not in the hedged item. [IFRS 9.B6.5.5]. Whilst this appears to be a logical requirement, it could have wider implications for cash flow hedges than many would have expected. An entity cannot simply assume no ineffectiveness for a cash flow hedge because the principal terms of the hedging instrument exactly match the principal terms of a hedged item, if there are differences in other features of the hedging instrument and the hedged item. For example, IFRS 13 requires an entity to reflect both the counterparty’s credit risk and the entity’s own credit risk when determining the fair value of a derivative. The same credit risk cannot be assumed in the hypothetical derivative. This difference in credit risk would result in some ineffectiveness (see sections 7.4.5 and 7.4.9 below). It is possible to exclude the credit risk in the hedged item from the hedge relationship, for example by designating interest rate risk as the hedged risk (see section 2.2 above). This does not eliminate ineffectiveness from changes in the credit risk of the hedging derivative but prevents additional ineffectiveness from changes in the credit risk of the hedged item. (This assumes that credit risk is not of a magnitude such that it dominates the value changes of the hedged item (see section 6.4.2 above)). Illustration 7-12 and Illustration 7-13 below provide simple examples of how an entity might establish and apply a hypothetical derivative for the calculation of ineffectiveness in a hedge relationship. Illustration 7-12: Determination of a hypothetical derivative in a cash flow hedge of interest rate risk Company X has purchased £200m of variable rate 5-year debt securities. The debt securities yield 1-month SONIA plus 100 basis points with coupons paid monthly. Company X wishes to mitigate cash flow variability from the debt securities by transacting a 5-year interest rate swap with £200m notional, receiving a fixed coupon of 2.0% quarterly and paying an overnight SONIA rate, compounded daily. The swap and the debt securities were acquired on the same day at prevailing market rates and designated in a cash flow hedge relationship for changes in 1-month SONIA, i.e., excluding credit risk from the debt securities. As the hedged item and the hedging swap reference different indices, there is likely to be a difference between the hypothetical derivative and the actual hedging swap. The hypothetical derivative representing the hedged risk in the issued debt might be determined as follows: A 5-year interest rate swap with £200m notional, receiving 1-month SONIA, and paying a 2.02% fixed coupon monthly. The fixed coupon of 2.02% is calculated such that the hypothetical derivative has a zero fair value on the date of designation. The 2bp difference with the variable leg of the actual hedging swap reflects the difference between overnight SONIA compounding daily and 1-month SONIA (see section 7.4.5 below for a discussion on the selection of an appropriate discounting curve for calculation of fair value changes in the hypothetical derivative). The fact that the actual hedging derivative is referenced to overnight SONIA and cash flows occur quarterly has no impact on the determination of the hypothetical derivative. The hypothetical derivative can only be based on features that arise in the hedged item, which attracts a monthly coupon. The difference between the hypothetical derivative and the hedging derivative is likely to lead to ineffectiveness (see section 7.4.1 above).
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting Illustration 7-13: Impact on ineffectiveness of changes in credit risk in the hedged item when the hedged risk is a benchmark rate component Entity A has issued floating rate debt, paying a benchmark floating rate plus a credit spread of 1%. Entity A also transacts a pay fixed, receive benchmark interest rate swap in order to eliminate variability in cash flows from changes in the benchmark rate. The entity designates this swap as a hedge of the benchmark rate component of the issued debt and excludes credit risk on the debt from the hedge relationship. Accordingly, if subsequently Entity A’s credit rating changes such that the current credit spread is now 0.95%, this will have no impact on the change in value of the hedged item for the purposes of measuring ineffectiveness, as credit risk in the hedged item has been excluded from the hedge relationship. Conversely, if there is a change in the credit risk with respect to the interest rate swap (either that of the counterparty or the entity itself), resulting in a change in the fair value of the interest rate swap, this would affect the measurement of hedge ineffectiveness. The credit risk associated with the interest rate swap includes both counterparty and own credit risk (see section 7.4.9 below). When considering the credit risk of the hedged item, any changes must not be of a magnitude that it dominates the value changes in the hedged item such that the hedge effectiveness requirements are no longer met (see section 6.4.2.B above). That is unlikely to be the case for a change in credit spread from 1% to 0.95%. One other example of when the features of the hedging instrument and the hedged item are likely to differ is when an entity hedges a debt instrument denominated in a foreign currency in a cash flow hedge with a cross-currency swap (irrespective of whether it is fixed-rate or variable-rate debt). IFRS 9 is explicit that when using a hypothetical derivative to calculate ineffectiveness, the hypothetical derivative cannot simply include an imputed charge for exchanging different currencies (i.e., the foreign currency basis spread) even though actual derivatives (for example, cross-currency interest rate swaps) under which different currencies are exchanged might include such a charge. [IFRS 9.B6.5.5]. Although cross-currency interest rate swaps are used to highlight the fact that foreign currency basis spreads must not be replicated in hypothetical derivatives, this issue is also likely to arise in other foreign currency contracts settled in the future. To address this, IFRS 9 includes the ability to account for the foreign currency basis spread as a cost of hedging (see section 7.5 below). In many cases where the critical terms of the hedged item are closely matched by a hedging instrument which had a zero fair value on designation, the hypothetical derivative is likely to have similar terms to the actual hedging derivative - subject to the known differences mentioned above (e.g., foreign currency basis spreads and credit risk). When hedging a one sided risk (i.e., to designate only changes in the cash flows or fair value of a hedged item above or below a specified price or other variable (see section 2.1 above)) additional considerations apply in determining an appropriate hypothetical derivative. Although the hypothetical derivative must be based on the features that occur in the hedged item, the hypothetical derivative must also reflect that one sided nature of the hedge relationship. [IFRS 9.B6.5.5]. This is usually achieved by the hypothetical derivative taking the form of the intrinsic value of a hypothetical option contract, matching the features of the hedged item where possible, but introducing a strike price above or below which the one sided risk arises. However, the time value associated with the hypothetical option contract is excluded from the hedge relationship as it is not a component of the hedged item (see section 7.4.11 below). For hedges of risks other than one sided risks, the hypothetical derivative is required to have a zero fair value on designation. In the case of hypothetical derivatives for one sided risks, the intrinsic value of the hypothetical option must be zero (see section 3.6.4 above for discussion on acceptable ways to calculate the intrinsic value).
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting Illustration 7-14 below provides some examples as to how to identify an appropriate hypothetical derivative in the hedge of a one sided risk. Illustration 7-14: Identifying an appropriate hypothetical derivative in the case of a one sided risk. Company AA wishes to protect itself against a reduction in the price of Commodity X. It has highly probably forecast sales of 100 tonnes in 6 months time. The current price of 100 tonnes of Commodity X is CU11. Company AA purchases a floor with an underlying of 100 tonnes of Commodity X, for net settlement in 6 months time. Company AA designates the floor as a hedging instrument in a cash flow hedge of a one sided risk of Commodity X. Set out below are three different scenarios each with a different strike price transacted in the actual hedging instrument. Scenario 1 Company AA chooses to protect itself against a fall in the price of Commodity X below CU8, and so transacts an out of the money floor with a strike price of CU8, at premium of CU0.5. The floor has zero intrinsic value, the premium reflects time value. The one sided risk is below CU8 so an appropriate hypothetical derivative would have a strike price of CU8, other terms of the hypothetical derivative must be based on the forecast sales in terms of timing and pricing features. Scenario 2 Company AA chooses to protect itself against any fall in the price of Commodity X (i.e., below the current price of CU11), and so transacts an at the money floor with a strike price of CU11, at a premium of CU1. The floor has zero intrinsic value, the premium reflects time value. The one sided risk is below CU11 so an appropriate hypothetical derivative would have a strike price of CU11, other terms of the hypothetical derivative must be based on the forecast sales in terms of timing and pricing features. Scenario 3 Company AA transacts an in the money floor with a strike price of CU13, paying a premium of CU2.75, made up of intrinsic value of CU2 and time value of CU0.75. If the floor was designated as the hedging instrument in a cash flow hedge of a one sided risk of Commodity X, the appropriate hypothetical derivative would have a strike price of CU11, i.e., the current market price for Commodity X and the level at which the intrinsic value reaches zero. The fact that the actual derivative is already in the money (i.e., has a positive intrinsic value) is not relevant for the hedged risk. The CU2 of intrinsic value represents changes in the price of Commodity X in the period prior to the hedge designation and so is not relevant for the hedge relationship. This would also be the case if Company AA had identified an existing in the money floor. In all three scenarios, the time value associated with the hypothetical derivative is excluded from the hedge relationship as it is not a component of the hedged item (see section 7.4.11 below). The issues in Illustration 7-14 above are also applicable in the determination of the aligned derivative when hedging a one sided risk and applying the cost of hedging (see section 7.5.1.A below). The hypothetical derivative is first determined at inception of the hedge relationship based on the critical terms of the hedged item and prevailing market rates at that time. However, should any of the critical terms of the hedged item change subsequently, e.g., timing, volume or pricing inputs, then the hypothetical derivative will need to be updated to reflect those changes. Although the terms of the hypothetical derivative are updated to reflect current expectations of the hedged item, it is still constructed based on market rates at inception of the hedge relationship. This is to ensure the cumulative change in fair value of the hedged item since inception of the hedge relationship is captured for measurement and assessment of effectiveness. [IFRS 9.6.4.1(c), IFRS 9.6.5.11(a)].
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting Illustration 7-15 below provides an example of a hypothetical derivative being updated due to changes in the hedged item. Illustration 7-15: Change in expectations with respect to the hedged item Entity Z, with USD functional currency, as at 1/1/20X2 expects to purchase 10 industrial ovens from a UK supplier. The price of the ovens will be determined in GBP and will be agreed on 30/6/20X2 when the purchase is expected to be confirmed, for delivery and settlement 2 months later. Z considers the purchase of the 10 ovens on 31/8/20X2 to be highly probable. On 1/1/20X2, the price for each oven is GBP5,000, and historically the GBP price has been very stable. The USD/GBP forward rate on 1/1/20X2 for delivery on 31/8/20X2 is 0.8. Z wishes to reduce its exposure to changes in cash flow (the purchase price) due to foreign exchange risk and so enters into a foreign currency forward contract to buy GBP50,000 and sell USD62,500 for settlement on
**31/8/20X2.**
It is assumed that the hedge qualifying criteria are all met (see section 6.1 above), and the foreign currency forward contract is designated in a cash flow hedge of the USD/GBP forward exchange risk in the highly probable purchase of 10 ovens on 31/8/20X2. As the critical terms of the hedged item and the hedging foreign currency forward contract match, and the forward contract was transacted with a zero fair value, the hypothetical derivative representing the hedged item will have the same terms as the actual hedging foreign currency forward contract. This will not always be the case as demonstrated in Illustration 7-12 above. The following month it becomes clear that only 8 ovens are required by Entity Z. In addition, the supplier is facing delays in its production line and so will not be able to deliver the ovens until 30/10/20X2. Given the changes in volume and timing of the hedged item, the hypothetical derivative must be updated accordingly. The hypothetical derivative must be updated to take the form of a foreign exchange forward contract as follows: to buy GBP40,000 and sell USD51,282 for settlement on 30/10/20X2. The notional of the hypothetical derivative has been reduced to reflect the purchase of 8 rather than 10 ovens. The USD/GBP forward rate of 0.78 applied to the forward contract still reflects market rates on 1/1/20X2 but for the new delivery date of 30/10/20X2. It can be seen that given the changes in expectations of the hedged item, the hypothetical derivative no longer matches the hedging derivative and so some ineffectiveness is likely to arise. Although prospectively it is likely that Entity Z would undertake a partial dedesignation of the hedging instrument to reflect the GBP10,000 of hedged items that no longer exists (see section 8.3 below). Illustration 7-16 (see section 7.4.6 below) contains a comprehensive example of the accounting entries for a cash flow hedge of interest rate risk in a forecast debt issuance using a hypothetical derivative for the calculation of ineffectiveness. 7.4.5 Discount rates for calculating the change in value of the hedged item The standard is clear that entities must consider the time value of money when measuring hedge ineffectiveness. This means that an entity must determine the value of the hedged item on a present value basis (thereby including the effect of the time value of money) (see section 7.4.2 above). [IFRS 9.B6.5.4]. Although capturing the effect of the time value of money is a key aspect of the application of hedge accounting, little guidance is provided on how to calculate the time value in the hedged item. By contrast, guidance is given on how to calculate the fair value changes for hedging instruments in various places: in IFRS 13 for those held at fair value through profit or loss (see Chapter 15), and in IAS 21 for non-derivative hedging instruments of foreign currency risk (see section 3.3.1 above). For example, a key consideration for market participants in calculating the fair value of derivatives is the selection of an appropriate discount rate. Although an interest rate swaps (IRS) might be referenced to one benchmark rate (e.g., 1 month SONIA) the appropriate discount rates for calculating fair value in line with IFRS 13 guidance, might be derived from a different benchmark rate (e.g., OIS for cash collateralised trades) if that is how market participants would calculate the fair value. Overnight index swaps (OIS) are interest rate swaps where the floating leg is linked to a benchmark interest rate for overnight lending. Market participants typically use OIS rates as a discount curve for cash collateralised IRS as they reflect the credit risk of such an instrument.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting The way in which the time value of a hedged item is captured can have a significant impact on the measurement of ineffectiveness. This is illustrated in the following two examples. Consider firstly the example of a fair value hedge of a fixed rate bond for changes in an eligible benchmark interest rate risk component (see section 2.2.3.A above). It is clear that in order to capture the change in value of the hedged fixed cash flows attributable to changes in the benchmark interest rate, the discounting curve would have to be based on the designated benchmark interest rate. For a fair value hedge of a specified interest rate, to choose any other discounting curve would reflect a risk other than the designated hedged risk. For such hedges, in order to reflect the hedged risk, that choice of hedged risk predetermines the discounting rate to be applied to the hedged item. Hence for a fair value hedge, unless the benchmark rate designated as the hedged risk is the same as the discounting curve applied to the hedging instrument, ineffectiveness will arise. This is the case even if the hedged fixed cash flows match exactly the fixed cash flows of the hedging instrument. Now contrast this with the example of a benchmark interest rate (e.g., SONIA) floating rate debt, swapped into a fixed rate of interest using a receive-benchmark rate, pay-fixed interest rate swap, designated in a cash flow hedge for changes in the benchmark interest rate. For the purposes of calculating the change in the value of the hedged item due to the hedged risk, the entity could use a hypothetical derivative (see section 7.4.4 above). When measuring the fair value of the actual and hypothetical interest rate swaps, the entity would start by using the specified benchmark rate forward curve to forecast the future floating cash flows. However, potentially the discount rate applied to those future cash flows might not be the same as that used for forecasting. In the case of a collateralised swap (for which the relevant discount rate is likely to be an overnight rate (e.g., OIS), unless the value of the hypothetical derivative is also calculated using an OIS discounting curve, even though the future cash flows might be the same, changes in value of the hypothetical derivative and the fair value of the actual swap will not be same - resulting in ineffectiveness. How we see it For those hedge relationships where the discounting curve is not predetermined by the hedged risk (e.g., a cash flow hedge of a benchmark interest rate, in contrast to a fair value hedge of a benchmark interest rate for which the discount rate must reflect the hedged benchmark rate) the standard is not prescriptive, and practice has evolved such that there appears to be an element of choice as to which benchmark interest rate is used as the basis for discounting in the calculation of changes in value of the hypothetical derivative used to reflect the hedged risk in a cash flow hedge. We believe this choice is limited to a benchmark interest rate. For example, it is not permissible to use a discount rate that would reflect the ongoing specific credit risk inherent in the hedging instrument, as this would reflect a feature of the hedging instrument rather than the hedged item (see section 7.4.4 above). Put differently, although it might be possible to choose the discounting benchmark rate for the hedged item to be that which is used for discounting purposes for the actual derivative, no such choice exists to reflect elements of the valuation of the actual derivative such as the credit valuation adjustment (CVA) in determining value changes in the hedged item (see Chapter 15 at section 10.3.2). Hence changes in the specific credit risk inherent in the hedging instrument will still result in ineffectiveness. However, if the hedging derivative is collateralised, the use of an OIS curve for discounting the hedged item could result in very little ineffectiveness. Similarly, the ability to choose a benchmark rate curve for discounting purposes when calculating a change in value of the hedged item, does not permit the inclusion of cross-currency spreads within such a curve - unless for a hedge of foreign currency risk and the cross-currency basis occurs in the hedged item (see section 7.4.4 above). Any choice of discounting benchmark rate must be documented at the inception of the hedge relationship (see section 6.3 above). As a result of the reforms mandated by the Financial Stability Board following the financial crisis, IBOR (including LIBOR) is being replaced by new ‘official’ benchmark rates, known as RFRs. IBOR Reform is ongoing, since whilst many IBORs have now transitioned to RFRs, some are temporarily continuing in synthetic form and some are still to transition. Such a change will necessarily affect both forecasting and discounting curves used to value financial instruments and could reduce the instances where there is a difference between discounting curves for the valuation of hedging derivatives and hedged items. However, this change in benchmark rates raises a number of accounting issues, including some related to hedge accounting. The IASB has published two amendments to IFRS 9 and other standards to address these issues: in September 2019, Interest Rate Benchmark Reform, Amendments to IFRS 9, IAS 39 and IFRS 7, and in August 2020 Interest Rate Benchmark Reform Phase 2, Amendments to IFRS 9, IAS 39, IFRS 7, IFRS 4 Insurance Contracts and IFRS 16 (see the Supplement: IBOR Reform).
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting 7.4.6 Comprehensive example of calculation of measuring ineffectiveness for a cash flow hedge of a forecast transaction in a debt instrument Illustration 7-16 below contains a comprehensive example of the calculation of ineffectiveness and associated accounting entries for a cash flow hedge of a forecast debt issuance. A hypothetical derivative is used to measure the present value of the cumulative change in the hedged expected cash flows from inception of the hedge in order to measure effectiveness (see section 7.4.4 above). [IFRS 9.6.5.11(a)(ii)]. Using a hypothetical derivative is one possible way of calculating the change in the value of the hedged item and is probably the most frequently used for cash flow hedges. [IFRS 9.B6.5.5]. Illustration 7-16: Measuring ineffectiveness and associated accounting entries for a cash flow hedge of a forecast debt issuance A forecast issuance of debt (or other interest-bearing liability or asset) for which the interest rate will be set when it is issued, creates a cash flow exposure to interest rate changes because the related interest payments will be based on the benchmark interest rate that exists when the forecast debt issuance occurs. In order to eliminate that cash flow variability with respect to market interest rates, it is common for entities to transact a forward starting interest rate swap in order to economically lock in the benchmark interest rate for the forecast debt issuance. The forward starting interest rate swap could be designated as the hedging instrument in a cash flow hedge of benchmark interest rate risk from the forecast debt issuance. In order to be eligible for hedge accounting the forecast debt issuance must be highly probable of occurring (see section 2.6.1 above). Fact Pattern The date is 1/1/X0 and it is highly probable that Company X will issue a €100,000 one-year debt instrument in six months on 30/6/X0. The instrument will pay fixed interest quarterly with principal due at maturity on
**30/6/X1.**
X is exposed to interest rate increases and establishes a hedge of the interest cash flows of the forecast debt issue by entering into a forward starting interest rate swap. The swap has a term of one year and will start in six months time on 30/6/X0 to correspond with the terms of the forecast debt issue. X will pay a fixed rate and receive a variable rate on the interest rate swap. X designates the forward starting interest rate swap as the hedging instrument in a cash flow hedge of the benchmark interest rate risk component (e.g., 3M EURIBOR) within the forecast debt issue. When the forecast debt is issued, the interest rate swap is closed out and settled in cash. The qualifying criteria for hedge accounting are all assumed to have been met (see section 6 above). X chooses to use the hypothetical derivative method in order to calculate ineffectiveness (see section 7.4.4 above). As only the benchmark interest rate risk component has been designated, any other factors such as X’s credit risk, are excluded from the hedge relationship, unless changes in such factors result in the debt being issued at a sub-benchmark rate (see section 2.4 above). For the purposes of this illustration, it is assumed that the debt is not issued at a sub-benchmark rate. Set out below are the benchmark zero-coupon and forward rates required in order to establish the hypothetical derivative and to undertake the measurement of ineffectiveness and associated accounting entries. Market data date Annual benchmark interest rates 31/3/X0 30/6/XO 30/9/XO
**31/12/X0**
31/3/X1 30/6/X1 1/1/X0 Zero coupon rates 2.00% 2.25% 2.75% 3.50% 4.00% 4.25%
Forward rates 2.00% 2.50% 3.76% 5.78% 6.02% 5.51%
31/3/X0 Zero coupon rates
3.00% 3.25% 3.75% 4.00% 4.25%
Forward rates
3.00% 3.50% 4.76% 4.75% 5.26%
30/6/X0 Zero coupon rates
4.25% 5.00% 5.50% 5.75%
Forward rates
4.25% 5.76% 6.51% 6.50%
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting
Explanation of benchmark rates The zero-coupon rates represent the interest rates that would be earned for principal amounts invested at inception in zero-coupon instruments without credit risk i.e., instruments where all interest is compounded and settled at maturity. For example, based on data as of 31/3/X0, a zero-coupon debt instrument issued on 31/3/X0 and having a maturity of 6 months (30/09/X0) will pay a zero-coupon interest rate of 3.25%. The interest rates are expressed as annual rates, by convention. Please also note that the rates used in this example correspond to compounded rates (as opposed to nominal rates). As a consequence, in the above example, based on data as of 31/3/X0, the coupon paid for a principal amount invested at 31/3/X0 in a zero- coupon debt instrument with a maturity of 6 months will be equal to (1+3.25%)^(6/12)-1 = 1.61%. 1.61% is the period-zero coupon rate (as opposed to annual rate) corresponding to the annual-zero coupon rate of 3.25% for a period of 6 months The de-compounding to derive a 6 month coupon rate was achieved by the ^(6/12) element of the calculation. Zero coupon rates are used to determine appropriate discount rates. Using again the benchmark data provided above, on 31/3/X0 the appropriate discount rate for a cash flow occurring on 30/9/X0 (i.e., 6 months later) would be 1.61%, as calculated above. Hence a cash flow of €100 occurring on 30/9/X0 would have a present value on 31/03/X0 of €100/ (1+1.61%) = €98.4. The forward rates for each period are sometimes called forward forward rates beyond the first period. They represent the annualised market rate of interest that would accrue and be paid at the end of a single period assuming investment at the beginning of the forward-starting period. They are also expressed as annual rates in this example. Forward rates can be derived from the zero coupon rates. For example, the 3 month forward rate starting on 31/3/X0 (i.e., in 3 months’ time) is (((1+2.25%)^(6/12)) / ((1+2.00%)^(3/12)))^(12/3)-1 = 2.50%, based on current market data (as at 1/1/X0). The formulae is based on the premise that it is financially equivalent to invest €100 at inception (1/1/X0) for a period of 6 months (applying the 6 month zero coupon rate) and to invest €100 at inception (1/1/X0) for a period of 3 months (zero coupon and forward rate are equal for this period) and reinvest principal plus interests for another period of 3 months (at the forward rate for this 3 month period). Forward rates are used to determine expected future cash flows. For example, the forward rate used to determine the expected cash flows of a future 3-month floating rate bond would be as follows: A 3 month floating rate bond is expected to be issued on 30/6/X0, the expectation on 1/1/X0 would be that interest payable on maturity at 30/9/X0 would be €100,000 x ((1+3.76%)^(3/12)-1) = €926. 3.76% is the 3 month forward rate of interest starting on 30/6/X0 (in 6 months time), but quoted on an annual basis, so there is a need to convert it into a discreet 3 month basis rate by de-compounding, which is achieved by the ^(3/12) element of the previous calculation. Calculation of the fixed rate of a debt instrument, based on market rates When a fixed interest rate debt instrument with payment of quarterly coupons is expected to be issued at a future date, its expected fixed rate can be calculated based on zero coupon rates. This fixed interest rate would be equal to the fixed interest of an interest rate swap (IRS) exchanging it against floating interest rates, where the floating interest rates would be based on the forward interest rates corresponding to each period - this fixed rate is commonly referred to as the “swap rate”. In practice, this implied or expected fixed rate is calculated by solving for the fixed rate of interest that is required to bring the fair value of the swap to zero, or the fair value of the fixed rate debt to par using the zero coupon rates for discounting purposes. This is an iterative approach, often referred to as bootstrapping, and is too complex to demonstrate here. For an interest rate swap or a fixed rate debt with interest settling every 3 months with a forward starting date on 30/6/X0, the implied fixed rate would be calculated so that the fair value of the swap was zero, and the fair value of the fixed rate debt was par at 30/6/X0, but based on current market data (as at 1/1/X0). In this example, using the above current market data (1/1/X0), and applying bootstrapping, the quarterly settled implied annual fixed rate on a 1-year fixed rate debt expected to be issued on 30/6/X0 would be 5.155%. This is quoted as an annual nominal rate, with the rate attributable to each quarterly settlement being calculated as 5.155%*3/12 = 1.289%. The fixed rate of an IRS with a forward starting date as of 30/06/X0, a maturity of 1 year and quarterly payments, would be the same. The intention of Company X is to lock in the expected fixed rate of its future issuance at this implied fixed rate of 5.155% by settling the forward starting swap with this fixed interest rate. Hypothetical derivative The first step in measuring ineffectiveness using a hypothetical derivative is to determine its terms, i.e., establish a notional derivative that has terms that match the critical terms of the hedged exposure and a zero fair value at inception of the hedging relationship (see section 7.4.4 above). An interest rate swap with a notional of €100,000 starting in 6 months time with a maturity of 1 year paying a fixed rate
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting (quarterly) based on market data at 1/1/X0, receiving a benchmark swap rate would be an appropriate hypothetical derivative in this case. The hedge is designated on the same day that the swap is transacted and, in this fact pattern, as no other sources of ineffectiveness exist, the hypothetical derivative also attracts the same fixed rate as the hedging swap of 5.155%. This will not always be the case, as an entity cannot include features in the hypothetical derivative that only exist in the hedging instrument, but not in the hedged item (see section 7.4.4 above). The fair value of the hypothetical derivative can then be used as a proxy to measure the change in the present value of the hedged item against which changes in value of the actual hedging instrument are compared in order to calculate ineffectiveness.

which case the annual rates need to be converted. For example, forecast floating rate coupon payable at 31/12/X0 = €100,000*((1+5.78%)^(3/12)-1) = €1,415
(2) Present value of net cash flows is calculated by discounting back to 1/1/X0 at the zero coupon rate for
the period. For example, the present value of the net interest cash flow expected at 31/03/X1 is calculated as follows: €184*1/((1+4.00%)^(15/12)) = €176.
(3) Only the interest cashflows from the hypothetical derivative are considered above for the purposes of
calculating the present value. This is because the theoretical cash flows described as principal in the fixed and floating legs offset exactly, and do not contribute to the present value of the derivative. For the purposes of this illustration, we have assumed that there are no sources of ineffectiveness, such that changes in the present value of the hypothetical derivative will match exactly fair value changes in the hedging swap. Therefore, we have not set out the cash flows and fair values of the hedging swap, as they are the same as that of the hypothetical derivative in this case. Measuring hedge effectiveness and accounting at 1/1/X0 No accounting entries are required at 1/1/X0, the hedged item is still only a forecast transaction, and the hedging swap was transacted for zero consideration, equal to its fair value. For cash flows hedges of floating rate debt for which the hedging derivative remains in place once the debt is issued, the effective portion of the hedging swap will continue to be remeasured. In that case the reclassification of that effective portion can in some cases be achieved by recognising changes in the clean fair value of the hedging derivative in the cash flow hedge reserve and the net interest payable/receivable recognised in interest expense (see section 7.2.2 above). Total 5,168 - - - (5,168) It can be seen that after the effects of cash flow hedge accounting, total interest expense paid was €5,168, rather than the actual €5,617 interest expense paid on the debt issuance at 5.617%, reflecting the lower interest rate locked in on 1/1/X0. It is also important to note that there is nothing left in the cash flow hedge reserve when the hedged cash flows have all occurred and will no longer impact profit or loss. One other thing to note is that the 5.168% yield achieved following hedge accounting is not the same as the fixed leg of the hedging swap of 5.155%. The difference arises mainly from the effect of discounting on the future cash flows of the hedging swap on 30/6/X0 which is never unwound as the swap is closed out on that date once the debt is issued.25 7.4.7 Worked examples of spot rate and forward rate designations, plus the application of the costs of hedging accounting It was explained in section 3.6.5 above that the spot and forward elements of a forward contract can be treated separately for the purposes of hedge designation. Further, that if only the spot element is selected, a further choice is available as to whether the excluded elements are accounted for as costs of hedging or not (see section 7.5 below). Illustration 7-17 below contrasts how the assessment of effectiveness and calculation of ineffectiveness must be applied for a hedge relationship depending on the choices made on designation. Illustration 7-17: Cash flow hedge of highly probable forecast purchase of inventory in a foreign currency Company A has the Local Currency (LC) as its functional and presentation currency. On 1 January 20X3, A enters into a forward exchange contract to receive Foreign Currency (FC) 100,000 and deliver LC109,600 on 30 June 20X3 at an initial cost and fair value of zero. On inception, it designates the forward exchange contract as a hedging instrument in a cash flow hedge of a highly probable forecast purchase of a certain quantity of paper for FC100,000 on 30 June 20X3. It is assumed that all hedge accounting conditions in IFRS 9 are met. The relevant foreign currency rates and associated fair values for the forward exchange contract are provided in the following table: Date Spot rate Forward rate to 30 June 20X3 Fair value of forward contract

1.075 - (2,100) For simplification purposes foreign currency basis risk is assumed to be zero. The applicable yield curve in the local currency is flat at 4% per annum throughout the period. The fair value of the forward exchange contract is negative LC1,980 on 31 March 20X3 ({[1.076 × 100,000] - 109,600} ÷ 1.04(3/12)), and negative LC2,100 on 30 June 20X3 (1.075 × 100,000 - 109,600).
If Company X had issued a floating rate debt (instead of a fixed rate debt) and had not early settled the IRS as of 30/6/X0 (but kept it until its 30/6/X1 maturity) , then the total amount of interests paid on the debt plus the net interests paid / received on the IRS would have been exactly equal to €5,155 (corresponding exactly to the ‘hedged’ fixed rate).
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting Case 1: Changes in the forward rate of the forward exchange contract are designated in the hedge relationship Ignoring ineffectiveness that might arise from other elements that have an impact on the fair value of the hedging instrument (such as foreign currency basis spreads (see section 7.4.8 below)), the hedge is expected to be fully effective because the critical terms of the forward exchange contract and the purchase contract are otherwise the same. The assessments of hedge effectiveness are based on the forward rate. The accounting entries are as follows.

LC LC Dr OCI - cash flow hedge reserve 1,980

1,980 To recognise the change in the fair value of the forward exchange contract between 1 January 20X3 and 31 March 20X3, i.e., 1,980 - 0 = LC1,980, in other comprehensive income (OCI) within the cashflow hedge reserve. The hedge is considered fully effective because the loss on the forward exchange contract, LC1,980, exactly offsets the change in cash flows associated with the purchase contract based on the forward price {([1.076 × 100,000] - 109,600) ÷ 1.04(3/12)} - {([1.096 × 100,000] - 109,600) ÷ 1.04(6/12} = -LC1,980. The negative figure denotes a reduction in the net present value of cash outflows and, therefore, effectively represents a ‘gain’ to offset the loss on the forward exchange contract in OCI. The second element of the above equation equals zero (i.e., (1.096 × 100,000 - 109,600) = 0). This reflects the fact that the forward exchange contract is on market and accordingly has a zero present value on designation. Where this is the case, the calculation could be simplified as follows:
**{([1.076 × 100,000] - 109,600) ÷ 1.04(3/12)} = -LC1,980.**

Financial instruments: Hedge accounting Case 2: Changes in the spot element of the forward exchange contract only are designated in the hedge relationship (costs of hedging guidance is not applied) Ignoring ineffectiveness that might arise from other elements that have an impact on the fair value of the hedging instrument, (such as foreign currency basis spreads (see section 7.4.8 below)), the hedge is expected to be fully effective because the critical terms of the forward exchange contract and the purchase contract are the same and the change in the forward element of the forward exchange contract is excluded from the assessment of effectiveness. The assessment of hedge effectiveness is based on changes in the present value of the spot element. For this purpose, the spot element is calculated as the foreign currency cashflow translated at the prevailing spot rate on assessment date, less the deemed local currency cashflow as per spot rates on designation date. The present value is then calculated by discounting the spot element from the date the hedged item is expected to occur back to the assessment date, using discount factors derived from the applicable local currency yield curve. Changes in value of the excluded forward element are outside the hedge relationship and will be taken to profit or loss.

LC LC Dr Profit or loss (forward element) 2,178

1,980
Cr OCI - cash flow hedge reserve (spot element)
To recognise the change in the fair value of the forward exchange contract between 1 January 20X3 and 31 March 20X3, i.e., 1,980 - 0 = LC1,980. The change in the present value of the spot element of the forward exchange contract is a gain of LC198= {([1.074 × 100,000] - 107,200) ÷ 1.04(3/12)} - {([1.072 × 100,000] - 107,200) ÷ 1.04(6/12)}, which is recognised in OCI within the cash flow hedge reserve. As noted above, the second element of the above equation equals zero (i.e., (1.072 × 100,000) - 107,200 = 0). This reflects the fact that the spot element of the forward exchange contract is on market and accordingly has a zero present value on designation. Where this is the case, the calculation could be simplified as follows:
**{([1.074 × 100,000] - 107,200) ÷ 1.04(3/12)} = LC198.**
The present value of the spot element of the forward exchange contract is calculated, as there is a need to consider the time value of money for effectiveness purposes (see section 7.4.2 above). The hedge is considered fully effective because the gain in the present value of the spot element of the forward exchange contract, LC198, exactly offsets the change in the purchase price at spot rates {([1.074 × 100,000] - 107,200) ÷ 1.04(3/12)} - {([1.072 × 100,000] - 107,200) ÷ 1.04(6/12} = LC198. The positive figure denotes an increase in the net present value of cash outflows and, therefore, effectively represents a ‘loss’ to offset the spot element gain on the forward exchange contract in OCI. However, as the forward element is not part of the hedged risk, the change in value of the forward element of the forward exchange contract, a loss of LC2,178 = 1,980 + 198, is recognised in profit or loss. The value of the forward element is calculated as the difference between the full fair value change in the forward exchange contract and the change in the present value of the spot element.

Case 3: Changes in the spot element of the forward exchange contract only are designated in the hedge relationship, while the forward element is treated as a cost of hedging Ignoring ineffectiveness that might arise from other elements that have an impact on the fair value of the hedging instrument, (such as foreign currency basis spreads (see section 7.4.8 below)), the hedge is expected to be fully effective because the critical terms of the forward exchange contract and the purchase contract are the same and the change in the forward element of the forward exchange contract is excluded from the assessment of effectiveness. The assessment of hedge effectiveness is based on changes in the present value of the spot element. For this purpose, the spot element is calculated as the foreign currency cashflow translated at the prevailing spot rate on assessment date, less the deemed local currency cashflow as per spot rates on designation date. The present value is then calculated by discounting the spot element from the date the hedged item is expected to occur back to the assessment date, using discount factors derived from the applicable local currency yield curve. Changes in value of the excluded forward element are outside the hedge relationship and are treated as a cost of hedging a transaction related hedged item. [IFRS 9.6.5.15, IFRS 9.6.5.16].

To recognise the change in the fair value of the forward exchange contract between 1 January 20X3 and 31 March 20X3, i.e., 1,980 - 0 = LC1,980. The change in the present value of the spot element of the forward exchange contract is a gain of LC198= {([1.074 × 100,000] - 107,200) ÷ 1.04(3/12)} - {([1.072 × 100,000] - 107,200) ÷ 1.04(6/12)}, which is recognised in OCI within the cash flow hedge reserve. As noted above, the second element of the above equation equals zero (i.e., (1.072 × 100,000) - 107,200 = 0. This reflects the fact that the spot element of the forward exchange contract is on market and accordingly has a zero present value on designation. Financial instruments: Hedge accounting The present value of the spot element of the forward exchange contract is calculated as there is a need to consider the time value of money for effectiveness purposes (see section 7.4.2 above). The hedge is considered fully effective because the gain on the spot element of the forward exchange contract, LC198, exactly offsets the change in the purchase price at spot rates {([1.074 × 100,000] - 107,200) ÷ 1.04(3/12)} - {([1.072 × 100,000] - 107,200) ÷ 1.04(6/12} = LC198. The positive figure denotes an increase in the net present value of cash outflows and, therefore, effectively represents a ‘loss’ to offset the gain on the forward exchange contract in OCI. Applying the costs of hedging guidance to the forward element of the forward exchange contract (see section 7.5 below), the change in the forward element (the change in value not designated as part of the hedged risk), a loss of LC2,178 = 1,980 + 198, is recognised in a separate component of equity. In this example, as the critical terms of the hedged item and hedging instrument match, the value of the forward element is calculated as the difference between the full fair value change in the forward exchange contract and the change in the present value of the spot element. Only the change in value of the forward element that relates to the hedged item can be accumulated in a separate component of equity, therefore where differences exist between the hedged item and hedging instrument, there is a need to calculate the appropriate forward element using an aligned instrument based on the terms of the hedged item (see section 7.5.1.A below).

107,500 To recognise the purchase of the paper at the spot rate (1.075 × 100,000 = LC107,500), remove the cumulative gain on the spot element of the forward exchange contract that has been recognised in OCI within the cash flow hedge reserve (198 + 102 = LC300) and the cost of hedging recognised in a separate component of equity (2,178 + 222 = LC2,400) and include them in the initial measurement of the purchased paper. The inclusion of the cost of hedging as part of the initial measurement of the purchased paper is consistent with the required treatment of such a transaction related hedged item. For a time-period related hedged item, such as an interest rate swap hedging interest rate risk in a debt security, the timing and presentation of the removal of amounts from the separate component of equity is different (see Illustration 7- 18 and section 7.5.1 below). [IFRS 9.6.5.11(d)(i), IFRS 9.6.5.15, IFRS 9.6.5.16].
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting Accordingly, the initial measurement of the purchased paper is LC109,600, consisting of the purchase consideration of LC107,500, a hedging gain of LC300 and the cost of hedging of LC2,400. This is equivalent to the 1.096 forward foreign currency rate achieved through the forward exchange contract.
LC LC Dr Forward exchange contract- liability 2,100
Cr Cash
2,100 To record the net settlement of the forward exchange contract. In all these examples we have ignored foreign currency basis risk in the forward rate (see section 7.4.8 below). However, if we were to consider it, additional volatility in profit or loss may arise in Case 1,as foreign currency basis is not part of the hedged risk therefore any fair value changes in the hedging instrument due to foreign currency basis are not replicated in the hedged item. In Cases 2 and 3 above, the calculation method for the forward element would by default also include any foreign currency basis, i.e., the ’residual’ after the spot element is deducted from the full fair value changes. However, in Case 3, foreign currency basis could be considered part of cost of hedging, similar to the forward element. [IFRS 9.6.5.16]. The example illustrates that the time value of money is relevant for the assessment of effectiveness even when the spot element is designated in a hedge relationship (see section 7.4.2 above). However, in many circumstances the effect of discounting the revaluation of the spot element might not be material. In the above example the strategy was to protect against changes in foreign currency rates until the date the forecast purchase is expected to occur, perhaps on the assumption that the resulting foreign currency payable will be settled on the same day. An alternative, perhaps more common, hedging strategy is to protect against changes in foreign currency rates until the date the payable is expected to be settled. It is usually possible to achieve hedge accounting for such a strategy. In fact, there are a number of ways in which hedge accounting could be achieved. One such approach would be to designate the hedging instrument in a cash flow hedge to protect against changes in foreign currency rates in cash flows associated with a highly probable forecast purchase. Although the hedge relationship will end when the purchase is recognised, the hypothetical (and aligned) instrument (see section 7.4.4 above and section 7.5 below) could reflect the timing of the expected settlement of the associated trade payable, and so will provide good offset to changes in the hedging derivative over the life of the hedge relationship. Once the forecast purchase is recognised and the hedge relationship has ended, the hedging instrument can then be designated in either a new fair value or cash flow hedge of the associated foreign currency trade payable for changes in foreign currency risk (see sections 5.1.2, 5.2.3 and 7.2.3 above). This would be a new hedge designation hence the issue of additional ineffectiveness associated with designating a hedging instrument that has a non-zero fair value might arise (see section 7.4.3 above). Illustration 7-18 below provides an example of the accounting for a cash flow hedge of a foreign currency trade receivable for changes in foreign currency risk. Alternatively, a choice could be made not to apply any further hedge accounting if the combination of accounting for the hedging derivatives at fair value through profit or loss and the IAS 21 retranslation of the trade payable provides sufficient natural offset (although fair value changes in the forward points are likely to result in some volatility in profit or loss). Another alternative when hedging the foreign currency risk of a highly probable forecast purchase to the expected settlement date of the associated trade payable is to designate a cash flow hedge of both the foreign currency forecast purchase and associated foreign currency payable at the time the hedging instrument is transacted. However, this dual designation, whereby the documented hedged item changes from a highly probable forecast transaction to a recognised monetary item, does bring some additional complexity to the hedge relationship. Specifically, there is a need to calculate a change in value for each hedged item and allocate an appropriate share of the change in fair value of the hedging instrument to each hedged item. The standard does not proscribe how to calculate the appropriate change in value of the hedged item and hedging instrument in a hedge of both the foreign currency forecast purchase and associated foreign currency payable. The IAS 39 Implementation Guidance includes an example of such a hedge, in which case all changes in value of the hedged item and hedging instrument are attributed to the ‘live’ element of the hedge relationship at any one time. [IAS 39.F.5.6]. So, until the forecast purchase is recognised, all changes in value are attributed to the hedge of the forecast purchase. Once the purchase is recognised and any remaining variability arises from the associated trade payable, all subsequent changes in value are attributed to the hedge of the trade payable. This approach is relatively simple, as it can be applied using only the actual hedged items and instruments, but it can result in an arbitrary allocation of the forward element (and foreign currency basis) of the hedging instrument to each hedged item, depending on prevailing market rates on the day the purchase is recognised.
Financial instruments: Hedge accounting Another potential method to assess the effectiveness and measure ineffectiveness in a hedge of both the foreign currency forecast purchase and associated foreign currency payable, is to notionally split the hedging instrument into the two phases of the hedge relationship and apply the usual hedge accounting requirements to each of the notionally split instruments (see section 3.6.2 above). For example: a forecast foreign currency purchase is expected to occur on 30/6/20X2, the associated foreign currency trade payable is expected to settle on 30/9/20X2, and the hedging instrument (a forward foreign currency contract) matures on 30/9/20X2. In order to assess the effectiveness and measure ineffectiveness in the hedge relationship, both the hedged item and hedging instrument could be represented by a notional foreign currency forward contract maturing on 30/6/20X2 and a foreign currency swap with principal exchange on 30/6/20X2 and 30/9/20X2. (The combination of these matches the full hedged item and hedging instrument). How we see it We believe either of the above methods to allocate the change in value of the hedged item and hedging instrument in a hedge of both a foreign currency forecast purchase and associated foreign currency payable to be acceptable. Notionally splitting the hedging instrument into the two phases of the hedge relationship is the more operationally complex of the two methods, as the accounting relies on instruments that have been notionally split. However, it does provide a more meaningful allocation of the forward element (and foreign currency basis) between the forecast purchase and the trade payable. As noted above, some natural offset in profit or loss does occur when hedging the foreign currency risk in a foreign currency trade payable with a foreign currency forward contract. However, Illustration 7-18 below demonstrates how the accounting could be applied if designation in a hedge relationship is desirable. Illustration 7-18: Cash flow hedge of foreign currency trade receivable Company X has the Local Currency (LC) as its functional and presentation currency. On 30 September 20X2, following the sale of inventory for FC500,000, X meets the criteria to recognize the associated revenue. Settlement for the sale is not due until 31 March 20X3, hence X also recognises a foreign currency trade debtor receivable due 31 March 20X3 at the transaction price of FC500,000. [IFRS 9.5.1.3]. X also enters into forward exchange contract to deliver Foreign Currency (FC) 500,000 and receive LC1,110,000 on 31 March 20X3 at an initial cost and fair value of zero. On inception, it designates the forward exchange contract as a hedging instrument in a cash flow hedge of the recognised foreign currency trade receivable. It is assumed that all hedge accounting conditions in IFRS 9 are met. The relevant foreign currency rates and associated fair values for the forward exchange contract are provided in the following table: Date Spot rate Forward rate to 31 March 20X3 Fair value of forward contract
